===Original===

> Ἔσται καὶ Σάμος ἄμμος, ἐσεῖται Δῆλος ἄδηλος.

===Chinese===

> 萨摩斯将化作沙滩，得洛斯将无影无踪。（Ἔσται καὶ Σάμος ἄμμος, ἐσεῖται Δῆλος ἄδηλος.）

## Introduction / 引言
===Original===

About three months ago﻿—that is to say, toward the end of May of this year of 1900﻿—the writer whose name appears on the titlepage received as noteworthy a letter, and packet of papers, as it has been his lot to examine. They came from a very good friend of mine, whose name there is no reason that I should now conceal﻿—Dr. Arthur Lister Browne, M.A. (Oxon.), F.R.C.P. It happened that for two years I had been spending most of my time in France, and as Browne had a Norfolk practice, I had not seen him during my visits to London. Moreover, though our friendship was of the most intimate kind, we were both atrocious correspondents: so that only two notes passed between us during those years.
Till, last May, there reached me the letter﻿—and the packet﻿—to which I refer. The packet consisted of four notebooks, quite crowded throughout with those giddy shapes of Pitman’s shorthand, whose ensemble so resembles startled swarms hovering in flighty poses on the wing. They were scribbled in pencil, with little distinction between thick and thin strokes, few vowels: so that their slow deciphering, I can assure the reader, has been no holiday. The letter also was pencilled in shorthand; and this letter, together with the second of the notebooks which I have deciphered (it was marked “III”), I now publish.
[I must say, however, that in some five instances there will occur sentences rather crutched by my own guesswork; and in two instances the characters were so impossibly mystical, that I had to abandon the passage with a headache. But all this will be found immaterial to the general narrative.]
The following is Browne’s letter:

===Chinese===

大约三个月前——就是说，今年1900年5月末——扇页上署名的这位笔者，收到了一封信和一包文件；他经手的文稿里，没有几件比这更值得注意。寄信的是我的一位至交，我没有理由再隐瞒他的名字——阿瑟·利斯特·布朗医学博士（Dr. Arthur Lister Browne），牛津大学文学硕士，皇家内科医师学会会员。不巧那两年我的大半工夫都待在法国，布朗又在诺福克行医，我去伦敦也没碰上他。再说，我们的交情虽是最亲密的那种，两人又都是出名的懒笔友：那几年统共只通过两回短简。
直到五月，那封信，连同那个包裹，到了我手上。包裹里是四本笔记本，从头到尾挤满了皮特曼速记那些晕人的笔画，成群结队，活像一伙受惊的蜂，悬在半空摆着种种轻狂的姿势。是铅笔涂的，粗笔细笔不大分，元音又少：所以它们的缓译，我可以向读者担保，绝不是件轻省差事。那封信也是铅笔速记；这封信，连同我译出的第二本笔记本（标着「III」），如今一并发表。
[不过我得说，有大约五处，句子被我自己的猜测撑着拐；有两处，字迹玄得实在没法认，我只得攔下那一段，攔下一头疼。但这些于全篇叙事无关紧要。]
下面是布朗的信：

===Original===

> “**Dear Old Shiel**﻿—I have just been lying thinking of you, and wishing that you were here to give one a last squeeze of the hand before I﻿—‘go’: for, by all appearance, ‘going’ I am. Four days ago, I began to feel a soreness in the throat, and passing by old Johnson’s surgery at Selbridge, went in and asked him to have a look at me. He muttered something about membranous laryngitis which made me smile, but by the time I reached home I was hoarse, and not smiling: before night I had dyspnoea and laryngeal stridor. I at once telegraphed to London for Morgan, and, between him and Johnson, they have been opening my trachea, and burning my inside with chromic acid and the galvanic cautery. The difficulty as to breathing has subsided, and it is wonderful how little I suffer: but I am much too old a hand not to know what’s what: the bronchi are involved﻿—*too far* involved﻿—and as a matter of absolute fact, there isn’t any hope. Morgan is still, I believe, fondly dwelling upon the possibility of adding me to his successful-tracheotomy statistics, but prognosis was always my strong point, and I say No. The very small consolation of my death will be the beating of a specialist in his own line. So we shall see.
> “I have been arranging some of my affairs this morning, and remembered these notebooks. I intended letting you have them months ago, but my habit of putting things off, and the fact that the lady was alive from whom I took down the words, prevented me. Now she is dead, and as a literary man, and a student of life, you should be interested, if you can manage to read them. You may even find them valuable.
> “I am under a little morphia at present, propped up in a nice little state of languor, and as I am able to write without much effort, I will tell you in the old Pitman’s something about her. Her name was Miss Mary Wilson; she was about thirty when I met her, forty-five when she died, and I knew her intimately all those fifteen years. Do you know anything about the philosophy of the hypnotic trance? Well, that was the relation between us﻿—hypnotist and subject. She had been under another man before my time, but no one was ever so successful with her as I. She suffered from tic douloureux of the fifth nerve. She had had most of her teeth drawn before I saw her, and an attempt had been made to wrench out the nerve on the left side by the external scission.

===Chinese===

> 「亲爱的老希尔——我刚躺着想起你来，盼着你在这儿，好在我『走』之前，再握一握手。——因为看样子，我是要『走』了。四天前，我开始觉得喉咙疼，路过塞尔布里奇，进老约翰逊的诊所，叫他给我瞧瞧。他咕哝了一句膜性喉炎，叫我笑了；可走到家，我已经哑了，笑不出来了；入夜，我呼吸艰难，喉头鸣响。我立刻打电报到伦敦请摩根；他们两个，一个约翰逊一个摩根，给我的气管开了口，拿铬酸和电烙铁烧我的里面。呼吸的困难退了；奇怪的是我竟没受多少罪：可我这只老手太老了，什么是什么，瞒不了我：支气管也牵进去了——牵得太深——实实在在说，没有指望了。摩根，我看，还在痴心盘算把我添进他气管切开术的成功统计；可预断向来是我的强项，我说：没门儿。我这一死的一点小小的安慰，是胜了一位本行的专家一角。走着瞧吧。
> 今早我把几件身后事理了理，想起了这几本笔记。几个月前我就想给你，一来我这人好拖，二来那位口授的太太还活着，就攔下了。如今她死了。你是弄文学的，又是研究人生的，这几本你若有本事读下来，该感兴趣。说不定还觉得有价值。
> 我这会儿打了点吗啡，撑在一小团慵懒里，写字还不很费劲，就用老皮特曼给你讲讲她。她叫玛丽·威尔逊小姐（Mary Wilson）；我认识她时她三十上下，死时四十五，那十五年我同她相熟。催眠恍惚状态的学问，你懂一点吗？我们两个的关系就是这个——催眠师和受术者。在我之前她跟过另一个男人，可谁也没我在她身上那样成功。她害第五对神经的三叉神经痛。我见她之前，牙已拔得差不多，左边那根神经还试过从外面切开往外拧。

===Original===

But it made no difference: all the clocks in hell ticktacked in that poor woman’s jaw, and it was the mercy of Providence that ever she came across *me*. My organisation was found to have almost complete, and quite easy, control over hers, and with a few passes I could expel her Legion.
> “Well, you never saw anyone so singular in personal appearance as my friend, Miss Wilson. Medicine-man as I am, I could never behold her suddenly without a sensation of shock: she suggested so inevitably what we call ‘the *other* world,’ one detecting about her some odour of the worm, with the feeling that here was rather ghost than woman. And yet I can hardly convey to you the why of this, except by dry details as to the contours of her lofty brow, meagre lips, pointed chin, and ashen cheeks. She was tall and deplorably emaciated, her whole skeleton, except the thighbones, being quite visible. Her eyes were of the bluish hue of cigarette smoke, and had in them the strangest, feeble, unearthly gaze; while at thirty-five her paltry wisp of hair was quite white.
> “She was well-to-do, and lived alone in old Wooding Manor-house, five miles from Ash Thomas. As you know, I was ‘beginning’ in these parts at the time, and soon took up my residence at the manor. She insisted that I should devote myself to her alone; and that one patient constituted the most lucrative practice which I ever had.
> “Well, I quickly found that, in the state of trance, Miss Wilson possessed very remarkable powers: remarkable, I mean, not, of course, because peculiar to herself in *kind*, but because they were so constant, reliable, exact, and far-reaching, in degree. The veriest fledgling in psychical science will now sit and discourse finically to you about the reporting powers of the mind in its trance state﻿—just as though it was something quite new! This simple fact, I assure you, which the Psychical Research Society, only after endless investigation, admits to be scientific, has been perfectly well known to every old crone since the Middle Ages, and, I assume, long previously. What an unnecessary air of discovery!

===Chinese===

> 可全不管用：地狱里所有的钟都在那可怜女人的下巴里滴答；她能撞上我，真是天意开恩。人们发现我的机体对她的，有差不多完全的、又极轻易的控制：几个手法，我就能赶走她的那一群鬼。
> 你从没见过相貌像我的朋友威尔逊小姐那样奇特的人。我虽是个行医的，每次冷不丁见着她，都不免一惊：她那样躲不开地叫人想起我们所谓的『彼岸』——你在她身上喫得出一股虫的气味，只觉得这与其说是女人，不如说是鬼。可我很难把所以然说给你听，只好干巴巴地说说轮廓：高额，薄唇，尖颏，灰白的脸颊。她高挑，瘦得叫人心里发惨，除了股骨，全身的骨架都看得见。她的眼睛是香烟烟雾的那种蓝，眼神里有一种顶古怪的、虚弱的、非人间的凝望；三十五岁上，她那一头寒碜的稀发就全白了。
> 她家道殷实，独自住在伍丁庄园老宅，离阿什托马斯五英里。你知道，我那时正在这一带『起家』，不久就住进了庄园。她坚持要我一心只管她一个病人；那一个病人，构成我行医以来顶肥的一份进项。
> 呃，我很快发觉：威尔逊小姐在恍惚状态里，有非常了不起的本事——了不起，我自然不是说性质上独她一份，是说程度上那样恒定、可靠、精确、及远。如今灵学里顶嫩的雏儿也会坐下来，跟你细讲心灵在恍惚状态里的传报之能——好像是什么新玩意儿似的！我告诉你，这个简单的事实——心灵研究学会查了无穷无尽的案子才认下它是科学——中世纪以来的每一个老太婆早就摸得透熟，我想，还要早得多。好一副多余的发现脸！

===Original===

The certainty that someone in trance in Manchester can tell you what is going on in London, or in Peking, was not, of course, left to the acumen of an office in Fleet Street; and the society, in establishing the fact beyond doubt for the general public, has not gone one step toward explaining it. They have, in fact, revealed nothing that many of us did not, with absolute assurance, know before.
> “But talking of poor Miss Wilson, I say that her powers were *remarkable*, because, though not exceptional in *genre*, they were so special in quantity﻿—so ‘constant,’ and ‘far-reaching.’ I believe it to be a fact that, *in general*, the powers of trance manifest themselves more particularly with regard to space, as distinct from time: the spirit roams in the present﻿—it travels over a plain﻿—it does not *usually* attract the interest of observers by great ascents, or by great descents. I fancy that is so. But Miss Wilson’s gift was special to this extent, that she travelled in every direction, and easily in all but one, north and south, up and down, in the past, the present, and the future.
> “This I discovered, not at once, but gradually. She would emit a stream of sounds in the trance state﻿—I can hardly call it *speech*, so murmurous, yet guttural, was the utterance, mixed with puffy breath-sounds at the languid lips. This state was accompanied by an intense contraction of the pupils, absence of the knee-jerk, considerable rigor, and a rapt and arrant expression. I got into the habit of sitting long hours at her bedside, quite fascinated by her, trying to catch the import of that opiate and visionary language which came puffing and fluttering in deliberate monotone from her lips. Gradually, in the course of months, my ear learned to detect the words; ‘the veil was rent’ for me also; and I was able to follow somewhat the course of her musing and wandering spirit.
> “At the end of six months I heard her one day repeat some words which were familiar to me. They were these: ‘Such were the arts by which the Romans extended their conquests, and attained the palm of victory; and the concurring testimony of different authors enables us to describe them with precision﻿ ﻿…’ I was startled: they are part of Gibbon’s *Decline and Fall*, which I easily guessed that she had never read.
> “I said in a stern voice: ‘Where are you?’

===Chinese===

曼彻斯特一个恍惚中的人能告诉你伦敦、或北京正在发生什么——这一类的确凿，当然轮不到舰队街一间办公室的精明来发现；学会把这个事实给大众钉死了，也还一步没有走近它的解释。说真的，他们揭示的东西，我们许多人从前就确凿地知道，一样新的也没有。
> 「不过，说到可怜的威尔逊小姐，我说她的本事『了不起』，是因为它们虽然在门类上不算奇特，在量上却那样特别——那样『恒』，那样『远』。我相信这是个事实：恍惚之能，一般说，多显在空间上，同时间相对——魂在今世漫游——它走的是一片平地——通常不以大升大降惹人注目。我想是这样。可威尔逊小姐的天赋特别在这一点上：她朝每一个方向游，而且轻易——只有一个方向除外——南北、上下、过去、现在、将来。
> 这是我渐渐发现的，不是一下。她在恍惚里吐出一串串声音——很难叫它说话，那吐语那样呢喃，又那样喉音，混着倦唇间一阵阵的喷气。这状态伴着瞳孔的紧缩、膝跳的消失、相当的僵直、一脸出神的迷狂相。我惯于在她床边一坐几个钟头，叫她迷住了，竭力去捉那梦幻的语言的意思——它从她唇间一口一口、扑扑簌簌地，用不紧不慢的单调吐出来。几个月的工夫，我的耳朵渐渐学会辨词；『帷幕为我也撕开了』；她那沉思与游荡的魂的去向，我也约略跟得上了。
> 六个月末的一天，我听见她念几句我熟的话。是这样：『罗马人即以此等方略拓其征服，登胜利之极峰；诸家记载相符，俾吾侪得而确述之……』我一惊：这是吉本《罗马帝国衰亡史》里的话，而我一眼就猜到，她从没读过。
> 我厉声问：『你在哪儿？』

===Original===

> “She replied, ‘Us are in a room, eight hundred and eleven miles above. A man is writing. Us are reading.’
> “I may tell you two things: first, that in trance she never spoke of herself as ‘I,’ nor even as ‘we,’ but, for some unknown reason, in the *objective* way, as *us*: ‘us are,’ she would say﻿—‘us will,’ ‘us went’; though, of course, she was an educated lady, and I don’t think ever lived in the West of England, where they say ‘us’ in that way; secondly, when wandering in the past, she always represented herself as being *above* (the earth?), and higher the further back in time she went; in describing present events she appears to have felt herself *on* (the earth); while, as regards the future, she invariably declared that *us* were so many miles ‘within’ (the earth).
> “To her excursions in this last direction, however, there seemed to exist certain fixed limits: I say seemed, for I cannot be sure, and only mean that, in spite of my efforts, she never, in fact, went far in this direction. Three, four thousand ‘miles’ were common figures on her lips in describing her distance ‘above’; but her distance ‘within’ never got beyond sixty-three. Usually, she would say twenty, twenty-five. She appeared, in relation to the future, to resemble a diver in the deep sea, who, the deeper he strives, finds a more resistant pressure, till, at no great depth, resistance becomes prohibition, and he can no further strive.
> “I am afraid I can’t go on: though I had a good deal to tell you about this lady. During fifteen years, off and on, I sat listening by her dim bedside to her murmuring trances! At last my expert ear could detect the sense of her faintest sigh. I heard the *Decline and Fall* from beginning to end. Some of her reports were the most frivolous nonsense: over others I have hung in a horror of interest. Certainly, my friend, I have heard some amazing words proceed from those wan lips of Mary Wilson. Sometimes I could hitch her repeatedly to any scene or subject that I chose by the mere exercise of my will; at others, the flighty waywardness of her spirit eluded and baffled me: she resisted﻿—she disobeyed: otherwise I might have sent you, not four notebooks, but twenty, or forty. About the fifth year it struck me that it would be well to jot down her more connected utterances, since I knew shorthand.

===Chinese===

> 「她答：『俺们在楼上头，八百一十一英里。一个人在写。俺们在读。』
> 有两件事我可以告诉你：第一，恍惚中她从不说『我』，连『我们』也不说，不知为什么，总用宾格说『俺们』：她说『俺们在』——『俺们要』『俺们去了』；当然，她本是位受过教育的淑女，我想她也从没在英格兰西部住过——那边的人才这样『俺们』长『俺们』短。第二，在过去的年代里游荡时，她永远把自己放在『上头』（地上？），越往回走，位置越高；讲眼前的事，她仿佛觉得自己『在』（地上）；至于将来，她回回都断言『俺们』在（地底下）多少英里『里头』。
> 朝后一个方向出游，像是有几道固定的界限：我说像是，因为我不能拿准，只是说，任凭我怎样使劲，她朝那个方向事实上从没走远过。三千、四千『英里』，是她形容『上头』距离时嘴边的常数；可『里头』的距离，从没越过六十三。通常她说二十、二十五。在将来这件事上，她像深海里的潜水人：越往下挣，压力越强，到不深的地方，阻力就成了禁令，他再也挣不下去了。
> 恐怕写不动了：关于这位太太，我原有许多要告诉你。十五年，断断续续，我就坐在她昏暗的床边，听她呢喃的恍惚！到末了，我这双内行的耳朵，连她最轻的一声叹都听得出意思。《衰亡史》，我从头到尾听完了。她的传报，有些是最无聊的胡话；另一些，我是悬着一颗又怕又痒的心听过去的。真的，我的朋友，我从玛丽·威尔逊那两片憔悴的唇上，听见过一些叫人瞠目的话。有时，单凭我的意志，我能把她一回回拴在我挑定的任何一个场景、任何一个题目上；有时，她魂灵的轻狂任性又闪开我、耍弄我：她抗拒——她不从：不然我寄给你的就不是四本笔记，是二十本，四十本。约莫第五个年头上，我想起：既然我会速记，把她较连贯的话记下来，倒是一件好事。

===Original===

> “The notebook marked ‘I,’1 which seems to me the most curious, belongs to the seventh year. Its history, like those of the other three, is this: I heard her one afternoon murmuring in the intonation used when *reading*; the matter interested me; I asked her where she was. She replied: ‘Us are forty-five miles within: us read, and another writes’; from which I concluded that she was some fifteen to thirty years in the future, perusing an as yet unpublished work. After that, during some weeks, I managed to keep her to the same subject, and finally, I fancy, won pretty well the whole work. I believe you would find it striking, and hope you will be able to read my notes.
> “But no more of Mary Wilson now. Rather let us think a little of A. L. Browne, F.R.C.P.!﻿—with a breathing-tube in his trachea, and Eternity under his pillow﻿ ﻿…”

[Dr. Browne’s letter then continues on a subject of no interest here.]
[The present writer may add that Dr. Browne’s prognosis of his own case proved correct, for he passed away two days after writing the above. My transcription of the shorthand book marked “III” I now proceed to give without comment, merely reminding the reader that the words form the substance of a book or document to be written, or to be motived (according to Miss Wilson) in that Future, which, no less than the Past, substantively exists in the Present﻿—though, like the Past, we see it not. I need only add that the title, division into paragraphs, etc., have been arbitrarily contrived by myself for the sake of form and convenience.]
---
(Here begins the notebook marked “III”)

===Chinese===

> 「标着『I』的那本，我看是最奇特的一本，属于第七个年头。它和另外三本的来历是这样：一天下午，我听见她用『读书』的腔调呢喃；讲的东西勾了我的兴味；我问她在哪儿。她答：『俺们在里头四十五英里：俺们读，另一个人写』；由此我断定，她在将来的十五到三十年之间，读一部还没出版的著作。此后几个礼拜，我设法把她拴在同一题目上，末了，我想，全书差不多都赢到手了。我相信你会觉得它惊人，盼你读得懂我的笔记。
> 玛丽·威尔逊，如今就说到这儿。不如想想A·L·布朗自己吧，皇家内科医师学会会员！——气管里插着呼吸管，枕头底下压着永恒……」
[布朗医生的信随后谈的一桩事，与本书无关。]
[笔者可以补一句：布朗医生对自己病情的预断果然应验，写罢上信两日，他就去世了。我如今把标着「III」的那本速记笔记的迻译刊出，不加按语，只提醒读者：这些字句，构成一部书或一份文件的内容——照威尔逊小姐的说法，那书将在将来写出，或以将来的事为因——那将来，同过去一样，实实地存在于现在——只是同过去一样，我们看不见。只再添一句：标题、分段等等，是我为体例和方便起见，自行安上去的。]
---
（标着「III」的笔记本由此开始）

## The Purple Cloud / 紫云
===Original===

Well, the memory seems to be getting rather impaired now, rather weak. What, for instance, was the name of that parson who preached, just before the *Boreal* set out, about the wickedness of any further attempt to reach the North Pole? I have forgotten! Yet four years ago it was familiar to me as my own name.
Things which took place before the voyage seem to be getting a little cloudy in the memory now. I have sat here, in the loggia of this Cornish villa, to write down some sort of account of what has happened﻿—God knows why, since no eye can ever read it﻿—and at the very beginning I cannot remember the parson’s name.
He was a strange sort of man surely, a Scotchman from Ayrshire, big and gaunt, with tawny hair. He used to go about London streets in shough and rough-spun clothes, a plaid flung from one shoulder. Once I saw him in Holborn with his rather wild stalk, frowning and muttering to himself. He had no sooner come to London, and opened chapel (I think in Fetter Lane), than the little room began to be crowded; and when, some years afterwards, he moved to a big establishment in Kensington, all sorts of men, even from America and Australia, flocked to hear the thunderstorms that he talked, though certainly it was not an age apt to fly into enthusiasms over that species of pulpit prophets and prophecies. But this particular man undoubtedly did wake the strong dark feelings that sleep in the heart; his eyes were very singular and powerful; his voice from a whisper ran gathering, like snowballs, and crashed, as I have heard the pack-ice in commotion far yonder in the North; while his gestures were as uncouth and gawky as some wild man’s of the primitive ages.
Well, this man﻿—what *was* his name?﻿—Macintosh? Mackay? I think﻿—yes, that was it! *Mackay.* Mackay saw fit to take offence at the new attempt to reach the Pole in the *Boreal*; and for three Sundays, when the preparations were nearing completion, stormed against it at Kensington.

===Chinese===

唉，如今这记性似乎颇有些坏了，不大济事了。比方说，「北极号」（*Boreal*）启程前夕，那位宣讲「再探北极是何等罪过」的教士，叫什么名字来着？我竟忘了！可四年前，这名字于我熟悉得如同我自己的名字一般。
远航之前发生的种种，如今在记忆里也渐渐云雾起来。我坐在这康沃尔别墅的露廊里，要把所经历的事写个大概——天知道为什么写，反正不会有任何眼睛读到它——可一提笔，偏就记不起那教士的名字。
他实在是个怪人，艾尔郡来的苏格兰人，高大瘦削，一头黄褐色的头发。他常趿一双粗皮便鞋、穿一身粗呢衣裳在伦敦街头走动，肩上斜披一条格纹毯。有一回我在霍尔本瞧见他，步子带着几分野性，眉头紧锁，自顾自地喃喃。他刚到伦敦开堂布道（我想是在菲特巷），那间小屋便开始人满为患；数年之后他迁入肯辛顿的一处大场所，各色人等，连美洲和澳洲来的都有，蜂拥去听他讲的那一场场雷霆风雨——虽说那实在不是个容易为这类讲坛先知与预言发热的年代。但此人确凿无疑能唤醒人心底沉睡的强烈而阴暗的情绪；他的眼睛出奇而有力；他的嗓音从耳语起步，滚雪球似的越滚越大，末了轰然炸裂，像我在遥远的北方听见过的骚动中的浮冰；而他的手势，又粗拙得像上古某个野人。
对了，这人——他到底叫什么来着？——麦金托什？麦凯？我想想——对，就是它！麦凯（Mackay）。麦凯认为「北极号」这回新的极点远征大可问罪；于是接连三个礼拜天，在筹备事宜临近完竣之际，他在肯辛顿对这事厉声痛斥。

===Original===

The excitement of the world with regard to the North Pole had at this date reached a pitch which can only be described as *fevered*, though that word hardly expresses the strange ecstasy and unrest which prevailed: for the abstract interest which mankind, in mere desire for knowledge, had always felt in this unknown region, was now, suddenly, a thousand and a thousand times intensified by a new, concrete interest﻿—a tremendous *money* interest.
And the new zeal had ceased to be healthy in its tone as the old zeal was: for now the fierce demon Mammon was making his voice heard in this matter.
Within the ten years preceding the *Boreal* expedition, no less than twenty-seven expeditions had set out, and failed.
The secret of this new rage lay in the last will and testament of Mr. Charles P. Stickney of Chicago, that king of faddists, supposed to be the richest individual who ever lived: he, just ten years before the *Boreal* undertaking, had died, bequeathing 175 million dollars to the man, of whatever nationality, who first reached the Pole.
Such was the actual wording of the will﻿—“the man who first reached”: and from this loose method of designating the person intended had immediately burst forth a prolonged heat of controversy in Europe and America as to whether or no the testator meant *the Chief* of the first expedition which reached: but it was finally decided, on the highest legal authority, that, in any case, the actual wording of the document held good: and that it was the individual, whatever his station in the expedition, whose foot first reached the 90th degree of north latitude, who would have title to the fortune.
At all events, the public ferment had risen, as I say, to a pitch of positive fever; and as to the *Boreal* in particular, the daily progress of her preparations was minutely discussed in the newspapers, everyone was an authority on her fitting, and she was in every mouth a bet, a hope, a jest, or a sneer: for now, at last, it was felt that success was probable. So this Mackay had an acutely interested audience, if a somewhat startled, and a somewhat cynical, one.
A truly lionhearted man this must have been, after all, to dare proclaim a point-of-view so at variance with the spirit of his age! One against four hundred millions, they bent one way, he the opposite, saying that they were wrong, all wrong!

===Chinese===

在这当口，世人对北极的热衷已达一种只能以「热病」来形容的顶点，尽管这个词远不足以道尽当时弥漫的那份奇异的狂喜与不宁：因为人类纯粹出于求知欲而对这片未知地域素来怀有的抽象兴趣，如今骤然间被一种新的、具体的利害放大了千倍万倍——一股巨大的金钱利害。
而这股新热潮的声调，已不复旧日那般康健：因为如今那凶猛的魔王玛门，也要在这件事上发话了。
「北极号」远征之前十年间，出发而后折戟的探险队不下二十七支。
这新狂热的秘密，藏在芝加哥的查尔斯·P·斯蒂克尼（Charles P. Stickney）先生的遗嘱里——那位时尚怪人之王，据信是有史以来最富的个人：恰在「北极号」启事之前十年，他死了，遗赠一亿七千五百万美元，给那第一个抵达极点的人，无论何国国籍。
遗嘱的字句如此——「第一个抵达的人」：由于这指认方式含糊，欧美两地顿时爆出一场旷日持久的争论：立遗嘱人所谓「第一个抵达」，究竟是否指首支抵达远征队的队长？但最后经最高法律权威裁定：无论如何，文件字面有效；凡第一只脚踏上北纬九十度之人，不论其在远征队中身居何位，皆有权领受这笔巨产。
总之，如我所说，公众的骚动已然升腾为实实在在的热病；而单说「北极号」，她筹备的每日进展都在报上被人巨细靡遗地讨论，人人都是她装备方面的权威，她挂在每个人嘴上，是一场赌注、一个希望、一句玩笑或一声冷笑：因为如今，人们终于觉得成功有望了。于是这位麦凯便拥有一班听众，虽则多少被吓着、多少带着冷眼，却个个听得攥紧了心。
此人毕竟堪称胆气如狮，竟敢宣扬一种与他时代精神如此相悖的见解！一人对四万万人，四万万朝一头弯，他朝另一头，说他们都错了，全错了！

===Original===

People used to call him “John the Baptist Redivivus”: and without doubt he did suggest something of that sort. I suppose that at the time when he had the face to denounce the *Boreal* there was not a sovereign on any throne in Europe who, but for shame, would have been glad of a subordinate post on board.
On the third Sunday night of his denunciation I was there in that Kensington chapel, and I heard him. And the wild talk he talked! He seemed like a man delirious with inspiration.
The people sat quite spellbound, while Mackay’s prophesying voice ranged up and down through all the modulations of thunder, from the hurrying mutter to the reverberant shock and climax: and those who came to scoff remained to wonder.
Put simply, what he said was this: That there was undoubtedly some sort of Fate, or Doom, connected with the Poles of the earth in reference to the human race: that man’s continued failure, in spite of continual efforts, to reach them, abundantly and super-abundantly proved this; and that this failure constituted a lesson﻿—*and a warning*﻿—which the race disregarded at its peril.
The North Pole, he said, was not so very far away, and the difficulties in the way of reaching it were not, on the face of them, so very great: human ingenuity had achieved a thousand things a thousand times more difficult; yet in spite of over half-a-dozen well-planned efforts in the nineteenth century, and thirty-one in the twentieth, man had never reached: always he had been baulked, baulked, by some seeming chance﻿—some restraining Hand: and herein lay the lesson﻿—*herein the warning*. Wonderfully﻿—really *wonderfully*﻿—like the Tree of Knowledge in Eden, he said, was that Pole: all the rest of earth lying open and offered to man﻿—but *That* persistently veiled and “forbidden.” It was as when a father lays a hand upon his son, with: “Not here, my child; wheresoever you will﻿—but not here.”

===Chinese===

人们常叫他「复活的施洗约翰」：无疑他确有几分那意思。我想，在他厚着脸皮痛斥「北极号」的当时，欧洲任何一位君主，若非碍于颜面，怕都乐意在船上谋个低微的职位。
他痛斥的第三个礼拜天夜里，我就在那肯辛顿的礼拜堂里，亲耳听见了他。他讲的那些狂言！简直像个人因天启而谵妄。
人们全然入定般坐着，麦凯那预言的嗓音在雷霆的一切调门里上下腾掷，从急促的低语到回荡的轰击与极点；来嘲弄的留下了，转而惊叹。
说得直白些，他的话是这样的：地球的两极与人类之间，无疑存在着某种命数，或曰劫数：人类屡试屡败、始终未能抵达，便充沛又超额地证明了这一点；而这失败构成一课——兼为一记警告——人类若置若罔闻，责任自负。
他说，北极并不那么遥远，抵达途中的困难，就表面看来也并不那么大：人类的巧智早已成就过千百件难上千百倍的事；然而尽管十九世纪有过七次以上筹划周详的尝试、二十世纪三十一次，人始终未能抵达：总被某种貌似偶然的什么——某只遏阻的手——挫败：而课就在这里——警告就在这里。他说，那极点像伊甸园里的知识之树，像得奇妙——真的像得奇妙：大地上其余的一切都向人类敞开、任人取用——唯独那一个，始终遮掩着、「禁止」着。就像父亲把手按在儿子身上，说：「这里不行，我的孩子；天涯海角随你去——唯独这里不行。」

===Original===

But human beings, he said, were free agents, with power to stop their ears, and turn a callous consciousness to the whispers and warning indications of Heaven; and he believed, he said, that the time was now come when man would find it absolutely in his power to stand on that 90th of latitude, and plant an impious right foot on the head of the earth﻿—just as it had been given into the absolute power of Adam to stretch an impious right hand, and pluck of the Fruit of Knowledge; but, said he﻿—his voice pealing now into one long proclamation of awful augury﻿—just as the abuse of that power had been followed in the one case by catastrophe swift and universal, so, in the other, he warned the entire race to look out thenceforth for nothing from God but a lowering sky, and thundery weather.
The man’s frantic earnestness, authoritative voice, and savage gestures, could not but have their effect upon all; as for me, I declare, I sat as though a messenger from Heaven addressed me. But I believe that I had not yet reached home, when the whole impression of the discourse had passed from me like water from a duck’s back. The Prophet in the twentieth century was not a success. John Baptist himself, camel-skin and all, would have met with only tolerant shrugs. I dismissed Mackay from my mind with the thought: “He is behind his age, I suppose.”
But haven’t I thought differently of Mackay since, my God﻿ ﻿… ?

===Chinese===

但他说，人类是自由的行动者，有能力捂住自己的耳朵，对上天的耳语与预警的征兆摆出一副麻木的意识；而他说，他相信时辰已到，人类将发现自已有绝对的能耐站上那第九十度纬线，把一只亵渎的右脚踩上地球的头颅——恰如当年也被赋给了亚当绝对的权能，可以伸出亵渎的右手，去摘知识之树的果子；但是——他说，他的嗓音此刻轰隆成一长声可怖谶语的宣告——正如前一例中那权能的滥用随即招来迅疾而普遍的大灾，在后一例中，他警告全人类：从此往后，休想再从上帝那里得到别的，除了阴沉的天，和雷雨的天气。
这人狂烈的恳切、威严的嗓音、野蛮的手势，不能不对所有人发生效力；至于我，我敢说，我坐在那里，仿佛有一位上天的使者正对我说话。可我相信，我还没到家，那篇讲道给我的全部印象，已如水过鸭背，涓滴不存。二十世纪的先知是不成功的。施洗约翰本人，连人带骆驼皮衣，也只会换来几声宽容的耸肩。我把麦凯从心里打发掉，想道：「他大概是落伍于他的时代了。」
可后来我对麦凯的想法，何尝还是这样啊，我的上帝……？

===Original===

Three weeks﻿—it was about that﻿—before that Sunday night discourse, I was visited by Clark, the chief of the coming expedition﻿—a mere visit of friendship. I had then been established about a year at No. II, Harley Street, and, though under twenty-five, had, I suppose, as élite a practice as any doctor in Europe.
Élite﻿—but small. I was able to maintain my state, and move among the great: but now and again I would feel the secret pinch of moneylessness. Just about that time, in fact, I was only saved from considerable embarrassment by the success of my book, “Applications of Science to the Arts.”
In the course of conversation that afternoon, Clark said to me in his light haphazard way:
“Do you know what I dreamed about you last night, Adam Jeffson? I dreamed that you were with us on the expedition.”
I think he must have seen my start: on the same night I had myself dreamed the same thing; but not a word said I about it now. There was a stammer in my tongue when I answered:
“Who? I?﻿—on the expedition?﻿—I would not go, if I were asked.”
“Oh, you would.”
“I wouldn’t. You forget that I am about to be married.”
“Well, we need not discuss the point, as Peters is not going to die,” said he. “Still, if anything did happen to him, you know, it is you I should come straight to, Adam Jeffson.”
“Clark, you jest,” I said: “I know really very little of astronomy, or magnetic phenomena. Besides, I am about to be married.﻿ ﻿…”
“But what about your botany, my friend? *There’s* what we should be wanting from you: and as for nautical astronomy, poh, a man with your scientific habit would pick all that up in no time.”
“You discuss the matter as gravely as though it were a possibility, Clark,” I said, smiling. “Such a thought would never enter my head: there is, first of all, my fiancée﻿—”
“Ah, the all-important Countess, eh?﻿—Well, but she, as far as I know the lady, would be the first to force you to go. The chance of stamping one’s foot on the North Pole does not occur to a man every day, my son.”
“Do talk of something else!” I said. “There is Peters.﻿ ﻿…”
“Well, of course, there is Peters. But believe me, the dream I had was so clear﻿—”
“Let me alone with your dreams, and your Poles!” I laughed.
Yes, I remember: I pretended to laugh loud!

===Chinese===

大约就在那个星期日夜晚的布道之前三个星期——差不多正是那个时候——克拉克（Clark）来探访过我，他是即将启程的那支探险队的队长——那不过是一次朋友间的造访。当时我在哈利街二号开业行医已有一年左右，虽然还不满二十五岁，但我想，我的主顾之精英，全欧洲任何一位医生都难出其右。
精英——然而寥寥。我还撑得起这份排场，周旋于显贵之间：但不时会暗暗感到手头拮据的窘迫。事实上，恰在那个时候，全靠我那本《科学在艺术中的应用》大为畅销，我才免于相当大的窘迫。
那天下午闲谈之间，克拉克以他那种轻飘飘、随口而出的样子对我说：
「你可知道我昨晚梦见了你什么，亚当·杰夫森？我梦见你跟我们一道去了探险。」
我想他必定看见我惊了一下：就在同一夜，我自己也梦见了同样的事；可此刻我一个字也没有提。我回答时舌头上带着结巴：
「谁？我？——去探险？——就算他们来请，我也不去。」
「哦，你会去的。」
「我不会。你忘了我就要结婚了。」
「好吧，这一点咱们不必争论，反正彼得斯又死不了，」他说，「不过，万一他真有个三长两短，你可知道，我头一个要找的就是你，亚当·杰夫森。」
「克拉克，你这是开玩笑，」我说，「我对天文学、对磁现象实在懂得很少。再说，我就要结婚了。……」
「可你的植物学呢，我的朋友？我们要的正是这个：至于航海天文学，呕，有你这样科学素养的人，一学就会。」
「你说得这么一本正经，倒好像真有这可能似的，克拉克，」我微笑着说，「这种念头压根儿进不了我的脑子：首先，我还有未婚妻呢——」
「啊，那位至关紧要的女伯爵，是吧？——嗯，不过就我对那位夫人的了解，头一个逼你去的准是她。把脚踏上北极的机会，可不是天天都落在人头上的，我的孩子。」
「快换个话题吧！」我说，「不是还有彼得斯么。……」
「嗯，当然，是有彼得斯。可相信我，我做的那个梦实在太清楚了——」
「饶了我吧，别再提你的梦、你的极地了！」我大笑。
是的，我记得：我那是佯装放声大笑！

===Original===

But my secret heart knew, even *then*, that one of those crises was occurring in my life which, from my youth, has made it the most extraordinary which any creature of earth ever lived. And I knew that this was so, firstly, because of the two dreams, and secondly, because, when Clark was gone, and I was drawing on my gloves to go to see my fiancée, I heard distinctly the old two Voices talk within me: and One said: “Go not to see her now!” and the Other: “Yes, go, go!”
The two Voices of my life! An ordinary person reading my words would undoubtedly imagine that I mean only two ordinary contradictory impulses﻿—or else that I rave: for what modern man could comprehend how real-seeming were those voices, how loud, and how, ever and again, I heard them contend within me, with a nearness “nearer than breathing,” as it says in the poem, and “closer than hands and feet.”
About the age of seven it happened first to me. I was playing one summer evening in a pinewood of my father’s; half a mile away was a quarry-cliff; and as I played, it suddenly seemed as if someone said to me, inside of me: “Just take a walk toward the cliff”; and as if someone else said: “Don’t go that way at all”﻿—mere whispers then, which gradually, as I grew up, seemed to swell into cries of wrathful contention! I did go toward the cliff: it was steep, thirty feet high, and I fell. Some weeks later, on recovering speech, I told my astonished mother that “someone had pushed me” over the edge, and that someone else “had caught me” at the bottom!
One night, soon after my eleventh birthday, lying in bed, the thought struck me that my life must be of great importance to some thing or things which I could not see; that two Powers, which hated each other, must be continually after me, one wishing for some reason to kill me, and the other for some reason to keep me alive, one wishing me to do so-and-so, and the other to do the opposite; that I was not a boy like other boys, but a creature separate, special, marked for﻿—something.

===Chinese===

可是我隐秘的心知道，即便在*那时*，我生命中那类危机之一正在发生——正是这些危机，使我的一生从少年时代起，成为世上一切生灵中所活过的最不平凡的一生。我之所以知道如此，一是因为那两个梦，二是因为克拉克走后，我正戴上手套要去看望未婚妻时，我清清楚楚听见那两个老声音又在我体内说话：一个说：「此刻不要去看她！」另一个说：「去，快去！」
我生命中的两个声音！寻常的人读到这些话，无疑会以为我指的不过是两种互相矛盾的寻常冲动——再不然就以为我在发呓语：因为现代人哪一个能领会，那些声音听来何等真切，何等响亮，而我又是如何一次又一次听见它们在我体内争斗，近得如诗中所说，「比呼吸更近」，「比手足更亲」。
大约七岁那年，这事头一回落到我身上。一个夏日傍晚，我在父亲的一片松林里玩耍；半英里外有一道采石场的峭壁；正玩着，我突然仿佛听见有人在我体内对我说：「往峭壁那边散散步吧」；又仿佛另一个人说：「千万那条路」——那时还只是耳语，随着我长大，渐渐仿佛涨成了怒目相争的呼号！我果然朝峭壁走了去：那壁陡立，有三十英尺高，我摔了下去。几个星期后，待我恢复说话，便告诉你骇的母亲，说「有人把我推」下了崖边，又有人在崖底「把我接住」了！
十一岁生日过后不久的一个夜里，我躺在床上，忽然想到：我的性命对某些我看不见的存在必定关系重大；有两个彼此仇恨的大能一直在追踪我，一个出于某种缘故想要我死，另一个出于某种缘故想要我活；一个要我行此事，另一个便要我行彼事；我不是一个寻常的男孩，而是一个分离的、特殊的生命，被标定了要去做——某事。

===Original===

Already I had notions, touches of mood, passing instincts, as occult and primitive, I verily believe, as those of the first man that stepped; so that such Biblical expressions as “The Lord spake to So-and-so, saying” have hardly ever suggested any question in my mind as to how the Voice was heard: I did not find it so very difficult to comprehend that originally man had more ears than two; nor should have been surprised to know that I, in these latter days, more or less resembled those primeval ones.
But not a creature, except perhaps my mother, has ever dreamed me what I here state that I was. I seemed the ordinary youth of my time, bow in my “Varsity eight,” cramming for exams., dawdling in clubs. When I had to decide as to a profession, who could have suspected the conflict that transacted itself in my soul, while my brain was indifferent to the matter﻿—that agony of strife with which the brawling voices shouted, the one: “Be a scientist﻿—a doctor,” and the other: “Be a lawyer, an engineer, an artist﻿—be *anything* but a doctor!”
A doctor I became, and went to what had grown into the greatest of medical schools﻿—Cambridge; and there it was that I came across a man, named Scotland, who had a rather odd view of the world. He had rooms, I remember, in the New Court at Trinity, and a set of us were generally there. He was always talking about certain “Black” and “White” Powers, till it became absurd, and the men used to call him “black-and-white-mystery-man,” because, one day, when someone said something about “the black mystery of the universe,” Scotland interrupted him with the words: “the black-and-white mystery.”
Quite well I remember Scotland now﻿—the sweetest, gentle soul he was, with a passion for cats, and Sappho, and the Anthology, very short in stature, with a Roman nose, continually making the effort to keep his neck straight, and draw his paunch in.

===Chinese===

我那时已有了种种念头、心绪的触感、转瞬即逝的本能，其幽秘与原始，我敢断言，不下于第一个行走于人间的始祖；因此诸如「耶和华对某人说话，说」一类的《圣经》套语，几乎从未使我对那声音是如何听见的起过疑问：我并不觉得难于理解，人最初耳朵本不止两只；倘若得知我在这些末世的日子里多少与那些太古先民相似，我也不会惊讶。
可是除母亲之外，没有一个生灵曾梦想到我在此自陈的本来面目。我看上去同当时寻常的青年无异：在大学八人艇上执弓，为考试临时抱佛脚，在俱乐部里消磨时光。到不得不择业的时候，谁能疑心到我灵魂深处正自行进行着一场争斗，而我的头脑对此漠不关心——那两个吵嚷的声音以何等苦痛的相争呐喊着，一个说：「做科学家——做医生」，另一个说：「做律师、做工程师、做艺术家——做什么都好，*就是*别做医生！」
我还是做了医生，进了当时已发展为医学首府的剑桥；正是在那里，我遇到一个名叫斯科特兰（Scotland）的人，他对世界抱有一种相当奇特的看法。我记得他住在三一学院新庭，我们一伙人常聚在他那里。他老是谈论什么「黑」的大能与「白」的大能，直到人人为之失笑，大家便叫他「黑白之谜先生」，因为有一天，有人谈起「宇宙的黑之谜」，斯科特兰插嘴道：「黑白之谜。」
如今我仍清清楚楚记得斯科特兰——他是个最温柔和善的灵魂，酷爱猫、萨福和《诗选集》，身材十分矮小，生着一只罗马鼻，总在不断努力挺直脖颈、收起肚腩。

===Original===

He used to say that the universe was being frantically contended for by two Powers: a White and a Black; that the White was the stronger, but did not find the conditions on our particular planet very favourable to his success; that he had got the best of it up to the Middle Ages in Europe, but since then had been slowly and stubbornly giving way before the Black; and that finally the Black would win﻿—not everywhere perhaps, but *here*﻿—and would carry off, if no other earth, at least *this* one, for his prize.
This was Scotland’s doctrine, which he never tired of repeating; and while others heard him with mere toleration, little could they divine with what agony of inward interest, I, cynically smiling there, drank in his words. Most profound, most profound, was the impression they made upon me.

===Chinese===

他常说，宇宙正被两个大能疯狂争夺：一个白的，一个黑的；白的大能更强，却发现我们这颗特殊行星上的环境对他的成功并不怎么有利；直到欧洲中世纪为止，他一直占着上风，但此后便在黑的大能面前缓慢而顽强地退让；最终黑的会赢——也许并非处处如此，但在*这里*如此——并且将掳走战利品：倘若不是别的什么地球，至少要掳走*这一颗*。
这就是斯科特兰的学说，他百说不厌；旁人听他，不过是姑且容忍，却很少有人猜得到，我坐在那儿挂着讥诮的微笑，是以何等痛切的内心关切啜饮着他的每一个字。它们给我的印象，深极了，深极了。

===Original===

But I was saying that when Clark left me, I was drawing on my gloves to go to see my fiancée, the Countess Clodagh, when I heard the two voices most clearly.
Sometimes the urgency of one or other impulse is so overpowering, that there is no resisting it: and it was so then with the one that bid me go.
I had to traverse the distance between Harley Street and Hanover Square, and all the time it was as though something shouted at my physical ear: “Since you go, breathe no word of the *Boreal*, and Clark’s visit”; and another shout: “Tell, tell, hide nothing!”
It seemed to last a month: yet it was only some minutes before I was in Hanover Square, and Clodagh in my arms.
She was, in my opinion, the most superb of creatures, Clodagh﻿—that haughty neck which seemed always scorning something just behind her left shoulder. Superb! but ah﻿—I know it now﻿—a godless woman, Clodagh, a bitter heart.
Clodagh once confessed to me that her favourite character in history was Lucrezia Borgia, and when she saw my horror, immediately added: “Well, no, I am only joking!” Such was her duplicity: for I see now that she lived in the constant effort to hide her heinous heart from me. Yet, now I think of it, how completely did Clodagh enthral me!
Our proposed marriage was opposed by both my family and hers: by mine, because her father and grandfather had died in lunatic asylums; and by hers, because, forsooth, I was neither a rich nor a noble match. A sister of hers, much older than herself, had married a common country doctor, Peters of Taunton, and this so-called mésalliance made the so-called mésalliance with me doubly detestable in the eyes of her relatives. But Clodagh’s extraordinary passion for me was to be stemmed neither by their threats nor prayers. What a flame, after all, was Clodagh! Sometimes she frightened me.
She was at this date no longer young, being by five years my senior, as also, by five years, the senior of her nephew, born from the marriage of her sister with Peters of Taunton. This nephew was Peter Peters, who was to accompany the *Boreal* expedition as doctor, botanist, and meteorological assistant.
On that day of Clark’s visit to me I had not been seated five minutes with Clodagh, when I said:
“Dr. Clark﻿—ha! ha! ha!﻿—has been talking to me about the Expedition. He says that if anything happened to Peters, I should be the first man he would run to.

===Chinese===

可是我方才说到：克拉克（Clark）离开我时，我正套上手套要去看我的未婚妻、克洛达赫伯爵夫人，就在这时，我把那两个声音听得再清楚不过。
有时一股冲动的逼迫如此压倒一切，简直无从抗拒：当时催我走的那一股便是如此。
从哈利街到汉诺威广场这段路，我一路走，一路仿佛有什么东西贴着我的肉耳喊：「既然要去，关于『北极号』和克拉克的来访，一个字也不许提」；跟着又一声喊：「说吧，说吧，什么也别瞒！」
这段路仿佛走了一个月：可其实不过几分钟，我就到了汉诺威广场，克洛达赫已在我怀里。
在我眼里她是造物中最最华贵的一个，克洛达赫——那高傲的脖颈，似乎永远在鄙夷着她左肩后头的什么东西。华贵！可是啊——如今我才知道——一个无神的女子，克洛达赫，一颗狠毒的心。
克洛达赫有一次向我招认，她在历史上最心折的人物是卢克雷齐娅·博尔贾；见我骇然，她随即又添一句：「呃，不，我不过是开玩笑！」她便是这样口是心非：因为我如今明白，她一直在苦心竭力地把她那颗狠毒的心对我隐瞒。然而，如今想来，克洛达赫又是何等彻底地役使了我！
我们拟议中的婚事，两边家庭都反对：我家反对，是因为她父亲和祖父都死在疯人院里；她家反对，则是因为，哼，我既不富又不贵。她有个比她年长得多的姐姐，嫁给了一个乡下医生、汤顿的彼得斯，这桩所谓门不当户不对的姻缘，使她家把与我之间的这桩「门不当户不对」看得加倍可憎。然而克洛达赫对我那份非常的情热，任他们威吓也好、哀求也好，全然遏止不住。克洛达赫到底是一团怎样的火焰啊！有时她叫我害怕。
这时她已不算年轻，长我五岁，也比她的外甥——她姐姐与汤顿的彼得斯婚后所生——年长五岁。这外甥便是彼得·彼得斯，将随「北极号」远征队出任医生、植物学家兼气象助理。
克拉克来看我的那一天，我在克洛达赫身边坐下还不到五分钟，就开口说：
「克拉克博士——哈！哈！哈！——同我谈了远征的事。他说，要是彼得斯有个三长两短，他要头一个来找我。

===Original===

He has had an absurd dream﻿ ﻿…”
The consciousness that filled me as I uttered these words was the *wickedness* of me﻿—the crooked wickedness. But I could no more help it than I could fly.
Clodagh was standing at a window holding a rose at her face. For quite a minute she made no reply. I saw her sharp-cut, florid face in profile, steadily bent and smelling. She said presently in her cold, rapid way:
“The man who first plants his foot on the North Pole will certainly be ennobled. I say nothing of the many millions﻿ ﻿… I only wish that I was a man!”
“I don’t know that I have any special ambition that way,” I rejoined. “I am very happy in my warm Eden with my Clodagh. I don’t like the outer Cold.”
“Don’t let me think little of you!” she answered pettishly.
“Why should you, Clodagh? I am not bound to desire to go to the North Pole, am I?”
“But you *would* go, I suppose, if you could?”
“I might﻿—I﻿—doubt it. There is our marriage.﻿ ﻿…”
“Marriage indeed! It is the one thing to transform our marriage from a sneaking difficulty to a ten times triumphant event.”
“You mean if *I* personally were the first to stand at the Pole. But there are many in an expedition. It is very unlikely that *I*, personally﻿—”
“For *me* you will, Adam﻿—” she began.
“ ‘*Will*,’ Clodagh?” I cried. “You say ‘*will*?’ there is not even the slightest shadow of a probability﻿—!”
“But why? There are still three weeks before the start. They say﻿ ﻿…”
She stopped, she stopped.
“They say what?”
Her voice dropped:
“That Peter takes atropine.”
Ah, I started then. She moved from the window, sat in a rocking-chair, and turned the leaves of a book, without reading. We were silent, she and I; I standing, looking at her, she drawing the thumb across the leaf-edges, and beginning again, contemplatively. Then she laughed dryly a little﻿—a dry, mad laugh.
“Why did you start when I said that?” she asked, reading now at random.
“*I!* I did not start, Clodagh! What made you think that I started? I did not start! Who told you, Clodagh, that Peters takes atropine?”
“He is my nephew: I should know. But don’t look dumbfounded in that absurd fashion: I have no intention of poisoning him in order to see you a multimillionaire, and a Peer of the Realm.﻿ ﻿…”
“My dearest Clodagh!”
“I easily might, however. He will be here presently. He is bringing Mr. Wilson for the evening.”

===Chinese===

他做了个荒唐的梦……」
说出这些话时，充塞我心头的是自己的邪恶意——那种歪心眼的恶。可我抑制不住，就像我不能飞。
克洛达赫站在窗前，手执一朵玫瑰贴在脸旁。足足有一分钟她不答话。我看见她那轮廓分明、血色鲜明的侧脸，低低地、定定地俯着，嗅着。片刻后她才以她那冷而急促的口吻说：
「第一个把脚踏上北极的人，笃定封爵。那几千万几千万我且不说……我只想，我若是个男人就好了！」
「我不知道自己在这方面有什么特别的野心，」我应道，「我在温暖伊甸园里陪着我的克洛达赫，已经很快乐了。我不喜欢外面的寒冷。」
「别叫我小看了你！」她偺然答道。
「怎么会呢，克洛达赫？我又没有义务渴望去北极，是不是？」
「可要是能去，你『会』去的，我想？」
「也许——我——说不准。我们还有婚约呢……」
「婚约，真是！正是这件事，能把我们的婚事从一桩偷偷摸摸的难题，变成一场十倍的凯旋。」
「你是说，倘若『我』本人第一个站上极点。可一支远征队里有那么多人。『我』本人——很难说——」
「为了『我』你会去的，亚当——」她开口。
「『会』，克洛达赫？」我叫起来。「你说『会』？连一丝一毫影子的可能性都没有——！」
「可为什么？离出发还有三个礼拜。他们说……」
她停住了，她停住了。
「说什么？」
她的声音沉了下去：
「说彼得在用阿托品。」
啊，我当时就一惊。她从窗边走开，坐进一张摇椅，翻动一本书页，却并不读。我们俩都沉默；我站着，望着她；她用拇指在书页边口上抹过去，又若有所思地从头抹起。随后她干笑了一声——一声干而狂的笑。
「我说这话时，你为什么一惊？」她问，眼睛此刻随意地扫着书页。
「『我』！我没惊，克洛达赫！你怎么会以为我惊了？我没有惊！谁告诉你的，克洛达赫，说彼得斯在用阿托品？」
「他是我外甥：我自然晓得。不过别那样傻头傻脑地发怔：我可没有意思毒死他，好看你当上千万富翁、当上贵族老爷……」
「我最亲爱的克洛达赫！」
「不过我轻易做得出来。他这就快到了。他今晚要带威尔逊先生来。

===Original===

(Wilson was going as electrician of the expedition.)
“Clodagh.” I said, “believe me, you jest in a manner which does not please me.”
“Do I really?” she answered with that haughty, stiff half-turn of her throat: “then I must be more exquisite. But, thank Heaven, it is only a jest. Women are no longer admired for doing such things.”
“Ha! ha! ha!﻿—no﻿—no longer admired, Clodagh! Oh, my good Lord! let us change this talk.﻿ ﻿…”
But now she could talk of nothing else. She got from me that afternoon the history of all the Polar expeditions of late years, how far they reached, by what aids, and why they failed. Her eyes shone; she listened eagerly. Before this time, indeed, she had been interested in the *Boreal*, knew the details of her outfitting, and was acquainted with several members of the expedition. But now, suddenly, her mind seemed wholly possessed, my mention of Clark’s visit apparently setting her well a-burn with the Pole-fever.
The passion of her kiss as I tore myself from her embrace that day I shall not forget. I went home with a pretty heavy heart.
The house of Dr. Peter Peters was three doors from mine, on the opposite side of the street. Toward one that night, his footman ran to knock me up with the news that Peters was very ill. I hurried to his bedside, and knew by the first glance at his deliriums and his staring pupils that he was poisoned with atropine. Wilson, the electrician, who had passed the evening with him at Clodagh’s in Hanover Square, was there.
“What on earth is the matter?” he said to me.
“Poisoned,” I answered.
“Good God! what with?”
“Atropine.”
“Good Heavens!”
“Don’t be frightened: I think he will recover.”
“Is that certain?”
“Yes, I think﻿—that is, if he leaves off taking the drug, Wilson.”
“What! it is he who has poisoned himself?”
I hesitated, I hesitated. But I said:
“He is in the habit of taking atropine, Wilson.”
Three hours I remained there, and, God knows, toiled hard for his life: and when I left him in the dark of the fore-day, my mind was at rest: he would recover.
I slept till 11 a.m., and then hurried over again to Peters. In the room were my two nurses, and Clodagh.
My beloved put her forefinger to her lips, whispering:
“Sh-h-h! he is asleep.﻿ ﻿…”
She came closer to my ear, saying:
“I heard the news early. I am come to stay with him, till﻿—the last.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

（威尔逊是随远征队出征的电工。）
「克洛达赫，」我说，「相信我，你这样开玩笑，我不喜欢。」
「真的么？」她答道，脖颈傲然、僵直地半转过去：「那我得更雅致些才是。不过，感谢上天，这只是句玩笑。如今的女人做这种事，可不再受人仰慕了。」
「哈！哈！哈！——是——不再受仰慕了，克洛达赫！噢，我的老天！咱们换个话题吧……」
可她如今别的什么都谈不进去了。那天下午，她从我这里把近年历次极地远征的历史都盘了去：到了多远，靠了什么，为何失败。她眼睛发光；听得急切。其实在此之前，她对「北极号」早有兴趣，装备的种种细节她都晓得，远征队里好几位成员她都相识。可如今，骤然间，她整个心神仿佛被攝住了，我一提克拉克的来访，显然把她烧得极点热病大发作。
那天我从她怀抱里挣脱出来时她那一吻的热情，我终生不忘。我回到家，心头相当沉重。
彼得·彼得斯医生的家离我家只隔三个门，在街对面。那晚一点钟光景，他的听差跑来把我敲醒，说彼得斯病得很重。我赶到他床边，一眼看他的谵妄和他瞪大的瞳孔，便知是阿托品中毒。电工威尔逊——那晚在汉诺威广场克洛达赫家陪他消磨了黄昏的——也在那里。
「这究竟是怎么回事？」他对我说。
「中毒了。」我答。
「天哪！中了什么？」
「阿托品。」
「老天爷！」
「别怕：我想他会好的。」
「有把握么？」
「有，我想——就是说，只要他停用那药，威尔逊。」
「什么！是他自己毒了自己？」
我踌躇了，我踌躇了。可我还是说了：
「他有服用阿托品的习惯，威尔逊。」
我在那里守了三个钟头，天知道为他的性命花了多大力气：到天亮前的昏暗里我离开他时，心里已安稳：他会好起来的。
我一觉睡到上午十一点，随即又赶回彼得斯家。房里是我的两名护士，还有克洛达赫。
我的爱人把食指竖在唇上，悄声道：
「噓——噓！他睡着了……」
她凑近我耳边，说：
「我一大早就得了信。我来守着他，直到——最后……」

===Original===

We looked at each other some time﻿—eye to eye, steadily, she and I: but mine dropped before Clodagh’s. A word was on my mouth to say, but I said nothing.
The recovery of Peters was not so steady as I had expected. At the end of the first week he was still prostrate. It was then that I said to Clodagh:
“Clodagh, your presence at the bedside here somehow does not please me. It is so unnecessary.”
“Unnecessary certainly,” she replied: “but I always had a genius for nursing, and a passion for watching the battles of the body. Since no one objects, why should you?”
“Ah!﻿ ﻿… I don’t know. This is a case that I dislike. I have half a mind to throw it to the devil.”
“Then do so.”
“And you, too﻿—go home, go home, Clodagh!”
“But *why*?﻿—if one does no harm. In these days of ‘the corruption of the upper classes,’ and Roman decadence of everything, shouldn’t every innocent whim be encouraged by you upright ones who strive against the tide? Whims are the brakes of crimes: and this is mine. I find a sensuous pleasure, almost a sensual, in dabbling in delicate drugs﻿—like Helen, for that matter, and Medea, and Calypso, and the great antique women, who were all excellent chemists. To study the human ship in a gale, and the slow drama of its foundering﻿—isn’t that a quite thrilling distraction? And I want you to get into the habit at once of letting me have my little way﻿—”
Now she touched my hair with a lofty playfulness that soothed me: but even then I looked upon the rumpled bed, and saw that the man there was really very sick.
I have still a nausea to write about it! Lucrezia Borgia in her own age may have been heroic: but Lucrezia in this late century! One could retch up the heart﻿ ﻿…
The man grew sick on that bed, I say. The second week passed, and only ten days remained before the start of the expedition.
At the end of that second week, Wilson, the electrician, was one evening sitting by Peter’s bedside when I entered.
At the moment, Clodagh was about to administer a dose to Peters; but seeing me, she put down the medicine-glass on the night table, and came toward me; and as she came, I saw a sight which stabbed me: for Wilson took up the deposited medicine-glass, elevated it, looked at it, smelled into it: and he did it with a kind of hurried, light-fingered stealth; and he did it with an under-look, and a meaningness of expression which, I thought, proved mistrust.

===Chinese===

我们对望了好一会儿——眼对眼，定定的，她和我：可我的眼光先从克洛达赫的眼前垂了下去。有个字眼已到我嘴边，可我什么也没说。
彼得斯的复原不如我预期的那样平稳。第一个星期过完，他还瘫在床上。就在那时我对克洛达赫说：
「克洛达赫，你守在这床边，我不知怎么不喜欢。这毫无必要。」
「毫无必要，那是自然，」她答道：「可我天生有看护的天才，又有一种观战的熱衷——观肉体的战斗。既然无人反对，你反对什么？」
「唉！……我不知道。这是个我不喜欢的病例。我真有点想把它扔给魔鬼。」
「那就扔吧。」
「你也一样——回家去，回家去，克洛达赫！」
「可『为什么』？——如果一个人不害人。在这『上流腐败』、样样罗马式頽废的年代，你们这些逆流而上的正人君子，不正该鼓励每一种无邪的任性么？任性是罪行的刹车：这就是我的任性。我在这微妙的药剂里淺着，感到一种感官的愉悦，几乎要算肉感的——这方面倒像海伦，像美狄亚，像卡吕普索，像那些个个都是出色药剂师的伟大古典女子。看一艘人的船在暴风里，看它慢慢沉没的长戏——这难道不是一桩够刺激的消遣？我要你从现在起就养成习惯，事事由着我的小性子——」
她此刻以一种高贵的顽皮碰了碰我的头发，那使我平静下来：可即便那时，我仍望向那凌乱的床，看见床上那人病得实在不轻。
如今写这事我还恶心！卢克雷齐娅·博尔贾在她自己的时代或许还算英雄：可卢克雷齐娅在这迟暮的世纪！能把心都呕出来……
那人在那床上病下去了，我说。第二个星期过去，离远征队出发只剩十天。
第二个星期末尾的一天晚上，电工威尔逊正坐在彼得床边，我走了进去。
当时克洛达赫正要给彼得斯服一剂药；一见我，便把药杯搁在床头小几上，朝我走来；她走来时，我看见一幕刺穿我的景象：因为威尔逊拿起了那只搁下的药杯，举起来，对着看，凑近了嗅：他做这事带着一种勿促的、指尖轻巧的隐秘；他做这事时眼皮下瞟，那副意味深长的神情，我想，分明是不信任。

===Original===

﻿ ﻿…
Meantime, Clark came each day. He had himself a medical degree, and about this time I called him in professionally, together with Alleyne of Cavendish Square, to consultation over Peters. The patient lay in a semi-coma broken by passionate vomitings, and his condition puzzled us all. I formally stated that he took atropine﻿—had been originally poisoned by atropine: but we saw that his present symptoms were not atropine symptoms, but, it almost seemed, of some other vegetable poison, which we could not precisely name.
“Mysterious thing,” said Clark to me, when we were alone.
“*I* don’t understand it,” I said.
“Who are the two nurses?”
“Oh, highly recommended people of my own.”
“At any rate, my dream about you comes true, Jeffson. It is clear that Peters is out of the running now.”
I shrugged.
“I now formally invite you to join the expedition,” said Clark: “do you consent?”
I shrugged again.
“Well, if that means consent,” he said, “let me remind you that you have only eight days, and all the world to do in them.”
This conversation occurred in the dining-room of Peters’ house: and as we passed through the door, I saw Clodagh gliding down the passage outside﻿—rapidly﻿—away from us.
Not a word I said to her that day about Clark’s invitation. Yet I asked myself repeatedly: Did she not know of it? Had she not *listened*, and heard?
However that was, about midnight, to my great surprise, Peters opened his eyes, and smiled. By noon the next day, his fine vitality, which so fitted him for an Arctic expedition, had reasserted itself. He was then leaning on an elbow, talking to Wilson, and except his pallor, and strong stomach-pains, there was now hardly a trace of his late approach to death. For the pains I prescribed some quarter-grain tablets of sulphate of morphia, and went away.
Now, David Wilson and I never greatly loved each other, and that very day he brought about a painful situation as between Peters and me, by telling Peters that I had taken his place in the expedition. Peters, a touchy fellow, at once dictated a letter of protest to Clark; and Clark sent Peters’ letter to me, marked with a big note of interrogation in blue pencil.
Now, all Peters’ preparations were made, mine not; and he had six days in which to recover himself.

===Chinese===

……
其间，克拉克天天来。他自己也有医学学位，大约这时我正式以同行的身份请了他，连同卡文迪什广场的艾林，一起会诊彼得斯。病人陷在半昏迷里，不时被剧烈的呕吐打断，他的病情把我们全都难住了。我正式说明：他服用阿托品——起初中的是阿托品的毒：可我们看出他目前的症状并非阿托品的症状，倒几乎像是另一种植物碱的中毒，我们说不出确切的名目。
「蹊跷，」克拉克单独对我说。
「『我』不明白。」我说。
「那两个护士是什么人？」
「哦，我自己逃选的、口碑极好的人。」
「无论如何，我关于你的那个梦应验了，杰夫森。现在很清楚，彼得斯退出了竞争。」
我耸耸肩。
「我现在正式邀你加入远征队，」克拉克说：「你答应么？」
我又耸耸肩。
「好吧，如果那就是答应，」他说，「那我提醒你：你只剩八天，而八天里要做完全世界那么多的事。」
这场谈话发生在彼得斯家的饭厅：我们穿门出去时，我暼见克洛达赫正在外面走廊里滑行——急急地——离我们远去。
那天关于克拉克的邀请，我对她一个字也没提。可我一遍遍自问：她莫非知道？她莫非『偷听』了，听见了？
无论怎样，约莫半夜时分，令我大吃一惊的是，彼得斯睁开了眼，微笑了。到次日中午，他那份如此契合极地远征的旺盛生命力又占了上风。他已用一只臂肘支着，同威尔逊谈话，除了面色苍白和剧烈的胃痛，先前那场濒死，如今几乎不留痕迹。我为那疼痛开了几片四分之一格令的硫酸吗啡，便走了。
咳，大卫·威尔逊（David Wilson）和我素来不大亲善，而就在那一天，他造成了我与彼得斯之间的一个尴尬局面：他告诉彼得斯说，我在远征队里顶了他的缺。彼得斯是个气性大的人，当即口授了一封抗议信给克拉克；克拉克把彼得斯的信转给我，上面用蓝铅笔标了一个大大的问号。
咳，彼得斯的一切准备都已停当，我的还没有；而离出发只剩六天，尽够他复原。

===Original===

I therefore wrote to Clark, saying that the changed circumstances of course annulled my acceptance of his offer, though I had already incurred the inconvenience of negotiating with a locum tenens.
This decided it: Peters was to go, I stay. The fifth day before the departure dawned. It was a Friday, the 15th June. Peters was now in an armchair. He was cheerful, but with a fevered pulse, and still the stomach-pains. I was giving him three quarter-grains of morphia a day. That Friday night, at 11 p.m., I visited him, and found Clodagh there, talking to him. Peters was smoking a cigar.
“Ah,” Clodagh said, “I was waiting for you, Adam. I didn’t know whether I was to inject anything tonight. Is it Yes or No?”
“What do you think, Peters?” I said: “any more pains?”
“Well, perhaps you had better give us another quarter,” he answered: “there’s still some trouble in the tummy off and on.”
“A quarter-grain, then, Clodagh,” I said.
As she opened the syringe-box, she remarked with a pout:
“Our patient has been naughty! He has taken some more atropine.”
I became angry at once.
“Peters,” I cried, “you know you have no right to be doing things like that without consulting me! Do that once more, and I swear I have nothing further to do with you!”
“Rubbish,” said Peters: “why all this unnecessary heat? It was a mere flea-bite. I felt that I needed it.”
“He injected it with his own hand﻿ ﻿…” remarked Clodagh.
She was now standing at the mantelpiece, having lifted the syringe-box from the night-table, taken from its velvet lining both the syringe and the vial containing the morphia tablets, and gone to the mantelpiece to melt one of the tablets in a little of the distilled water there. Her back was turned upon us, and she was a long time. I was standing; Peters in his armchair, smoking. Clodagh then began to talk about a Charity Bazaar which she had visited that afternoon.
She was long, she was long. The crazy thought passed through some dim region of my soul: “Why is she so *long*?”
“Ah, that was a pain!” went Peters: “never mind the bazaar, aunt﻿—think of the morphia.”
Suddenly an irresistible impulse seized me﻿—to rush upon her, to dash syringe, tabloids, glass, and all, from her hands. I *must* have obeyed it﻿—I was on the tip-top point of obeying﻿—my body already leant prone: but at that instant a voice at the opened door behind me said:
“Well, how is everything?”

===Chinese===

于是我写信给克拉克，说情形既变，我对他的允诺自然作废，虽然我已蒙不便，接洽了一位代理医生。
这一下定了局：彼得斯去，我留。启程前第五天破晓了。那是个礼拜五，六月十五日。彼得斯这时已坐在安乐椅里。他情绪很好，但脉象带热，胃痛也还在。我每天给他三份四分之一格令的吗啡。那个礼拜五夜里十一点，我去看他，见克洛达赫在那里陪他说话。彼得斯正抽着一支雪茄。
「啊，」克洛达赫说，「我在等你，亚当。我拿不准今晚要不要注射。打，还是不打？」
「你说呢，彼得斯？」我说：「还疼么？」
「唔，你最好还是再给我们来一份四分之一格令，」他答：「肚子里零零星星还有些麻烦。」
「那就四分之一格令，克洛达赫。」我说。
她打开针盒，噘着嘴议论道：
「我们的病人不乖！他又用了些阿托品。」
我立刻火了。
「彼得斯，」我叫道，「你知道，不问过我，你无权干这种事！再犯一回，我发誓从此不再管你！」
「废话，」彼得斯说：「何必动这么大的气？不过给跳蚤咬了一口。我觉得我需要它。」
「他自己动手注射的……」克洛达赫说道。
她此刻站在壁炉台前——她已把针盒从床头小几上端起，从丝绒衬里里取出注射器和装吗啡药片的小瓶，走到壁炉台前，就那儿的蒸馏水化开一片药。她背对着我们，好一阵好一阵。我站着；彼得斯坐在安乐椅里，抽着烟。克洛达赫这时开始谈她那天下午去过的一个义卖会。
她好慢，她好慢。一个疯狂的念头从我灵魂某处昏昧的地带掠过：「她为什么这么『慢』？」
「啊哟，疼了一下！」彼得斯说：「姨妈，别管什么义卖会了——想想吗啡吧。」
突然，一股不可抗拒的冲动攝住了我——冲上去，把注射器、药片、杯子，连同一切，从她手里打落。我几乎必定要服从它了——我已到了服从的顶点——我的身子已经前倾：可就在那一瞬，背后开着的门口有个声音说：
「喂，一切都好吗？」

===Original===

It was Wilson, the electrician, who stood there. With lightning swiftness I remembered an under-look of mistrust which I had once seen on his face. Oh, well, I would not, and could not!﻿—she was my love﻿—I stood like marble﻿ ﻿…
Clodagh went to meet Wilson with frank right hand, in the left being the fragile glass containing the injection. My eyes were fastened on her face: it was full of reassurance, of free innocence. I said to myself: “I must surely be mad!”
An ordinary chat began, while Clodagh turned up Peters’ sleeve, and, kneeling there, injected his forearm. As she rose, laughing at something said by Wilson, the drug-glass dropped from her hand, and her heel, by an apparent accident, trod on it. She put the syringe among a number of others on the mantelpiece.
“Your friend has been naughty, Mr. Wilson,” she said again with that same pout: “he has been taking more atropine.”
“Not really?” said Wilson.
“Let me alone, the whole of you,” answered Peters: “I ain’t a child.”
These were the last intelligible words he ever spoke. He died shortly before 1 a.m. He had been poisoned by a powerful dose of atropine.
From that moment to the moment when the *Boreal* bore me down the Thames, all the world was a mere tumbling nightmare to me, of which hardly any detail remains in my memory. Only I remember the inquest, and how I was called upon to prove that Peters had himself injected himself with atropine. This was corroborated by Wilson, and by Clodagh: and the verdict was in accordance.
And in all that chaotic hurry of preparation, three other things only, but those with clear distinctness now, I remember.
The first﻿—and chief﻿—is that tempest of words which I heard at Kensington from that big-mouthed Mackay on the Sunday night. What was it that led me, busy as I was, to that chapel that night? Well, perhaps I know.
There I sat, and heard him: and most strangely have those words of his peroration planted themselves in my brain, when, rising to a passion of prophecy, he shouted: “And as in the one case, transgression was followed by catastrophe swift and universal, so, in the other, I warn the entire race to look out thenceforth for nothing from God but a lowering sky, and thundery weather.”

===Chinese===

来的是电工威尔逊。闪电似的，我想起先前在他脸上见过的一副不信任的眼色。唉，罢了，我不肯，也不能！——她是我的爱人——我站着，像大理石……
克洛达赫迎向威尔逊，坦荡荡地伸出右手，左手里托着盛注射液的脆玻璃杯。我的双眼钉在她脸上：那脸上满是安然，是毫无芥蒂的天真。我对自己说：「我一定是疯了！」
一场寻常的闲谈开始了，其间克洛达赫捋起彼得的袖子，跪在那里，往他前臂注射。她起身时，正为威尔逊的一句话笑着，药杯从她手里跌落，她的鞋跟，看去像失手一般，踏了上去。她把注射器插回壁炉台上一排注射器中间。
「你的朋友不乖，威尔逊先生，」她又噘着嘴说：「他又用阿托品了。」
「不会吧？」威尔逊说。
「你们统统别管我，」彼得斯答：「我又不是小孩子。」
这是他生前最后一句囫囵话。他死于凌晨一点前不久。中的是烈性剂量阿托品的毒。
从那一刻起，到「北极号」载我顺泰晤士河而下那一刻止，整个世界于我只是场翻滚的噩梦，其中几乎没有任何细节留在我的记忆里。我只记得验尸审讯，记得怎样轮到我出来证明：彼得斯是自己给自己注射的阿托品。威尔逊和克洛达赫都作了证：裁定与此相符。
而在那一片乱糟糟的筹备之中，只有另外三件事——如今想来，桩桩分明——我还记得。
第一件——也是主要的——是那个礼拜天夜里我在肯辛顿听那大嗓门的麦凯（Mackay）讲的那场言辞的风暴。是什么引着我——忙成那样——那晚去了那礼拜堂呢？唉，也许我知道。
我坐在那里，听他讲：而他结束语里那些话，何等奇怪地在我脑子里生了根，当时他升到预言的狂热，吼道：「正如前一例，僭越随即招来迅疾而普遍的大灾；在后一例，我警告全人类：从此往后，休想再从上帝那里得到别的，除了阴沉的天，和雷雨的天气。」

===Original===

And this second thing I remember: that on reaching home, I walked into my disordered library (for I had had to hunt out some books), where I met my housekeeper in the act of rearranging things. She had apparently lifted an old Bible by the front cover to fling it on the table, for as I threw myself into a chair my eye fell upon the open print near the beginning. The print was very large, and a shaded lamp cast a light upon it. I had been hearing Mackay’s wild comparison of the Pole with the tree of Eden, and that no doubt was the reason why such a start convulsed me: for my listless eyes had chanced to rest upon some words.
“The woman gave me of the tree, and I did eat.﻿ ﻿…”
And a third thing I remember in all that turmoil of doubt and flurry: that as the ship moved down with the afternoon tide a telegram was put into my hand; it was a last word from Clodagh; and she said only this:

> “Be first﻿—for Me.”

===Chinese===

我记得的第二件事：到家后我走进凌乱的书房（因为我不得不翻找过一些书），撞见我的管家正在收拾。她显然刚捏着一本旧圣经的封面把它拎起来要丢到桌上，因为我往椅子里一坐，目光正落在开头不远处摊开的印页上。那印的字很大，一盏罩灯照着它。我刚听完麦凯把极点比作伊甸园之树的狂言，无疑正因如此，那一惊才使我浑身发颤：因为我倦怠的眼睛，恰巧歇在了几行字上。
「女人把那树上的果子给我，我就吃了……」
在那惶惑与慌乱的一片搅扰中，我记得的第三件事：船趁午后的潮水下行时，一封电报递到我手里；那是克洛达赫最后的一句话；她只说了这一句：

> 「争第一——为了我。」

===Original===

The *Boreal* left St. Katherine’s Docks in beautiful weather on the afternoon of the 19th June, full of good hope, bound for the Pole.
All about the docks was one region of heads stretched far in innumerable vagueness, and down the river to Woolwich a continuous dull roar and murmur of bees droned from both banks to cheer our departure.
The expedition was partly a national affair, subvented by Government: and if ever ship was well-found it was the *Boreal*. She had a frame tougher far than any battleship’s, capable of ramming some ten yards of drift-ice; and she was stuffed with sufficient pemmican, codroe, fish-meal, and so on, to last us not less than six years.
We were seventeen, all told, the five Heads (so to speak) of the undertaking being Clark (our Chief), John Mew (commander), Aubrey Maitland (meteorologist), Wilson (electrician), and myself (doctor, botanist, and assistant meteorologist).
The idea was to get as far east as the 100°, or the 120°, of longitude; to catch there the northern current; to push and drift our way northward; and when the ship could no further penetrate, to leave her (either three, or else four, of us, on ski), and with sledges drawn by dogs and reindeer make a dash for the Pole.
This had also been the plan of the last expedition﻿—that of the *Nix*﻿—and of several others. The *Boreal* only differed from the *Nix*, and others, in that she was a thing of nicer design, and of more exquisite forethought.
Our voyage was without incident up to the end of July, when we encountered a drift of ice-floes. On the 1st August we were at Kabarova, where we met our coal-ship, and took in a little coal for emergency, liquid air being our proper motor; also forty-three dogs, four reindeer, and a quantity of reindeer-moss; and two days later we turned our bows finally northward and eastward, passing through heavy “slack” ice under sail and liquid air in crisp weather, till, on the 27th August, we lay moored to a floe off the desolate island of Taimur.
The first thing which we saw here was a bear on the shore, watching for young whitefish: and promptly Clark, Mew, and Lamburn (engineer) went on shore in the launch, I and Maitland following in the pram, each party with three dogs.

===Chinese===

6月19日下午，天气晴好，*博雷尔*号（*Boreal*）驶离圣凯瑟琳船坞，满载着美好的希望，朝北极进发。
船坞四周是一片人头的海洋，茫茫无边，一直铺展开去；沿河直到伍利奇，两岸不断传来沉闷的轰鸣和蜂群似的嗡嗡声，为我们的启程送行。
这次探险多少算得一桩国家事业，由政府资助：而要说装备精良，天底下再没有哪条船比得上*博雷尔*号。她的船体比任何战舰都坚固得多，能撞开十来码厚的浮冰；舱里塞满了干肉饼、鳕鱼籽、鱼粉等等，足够我们吃上不少于六年。
我们总共十七人，这项事业的五位头目（姑且这么说）是：克拉克（我们的队长）、约翰·缪（John Mew，指挥官）、奥布里·梅特兰（Aubrey Maitland，气象学家）、威尔逊（电气师），以及我本人（医生、植物学家兼助理气象学家）。
计划是向东行进到东经100度乃至120度；在那里赶上北向的洋流；一路破冰漂流向北推进；待船再也无法前行时，留下船（我们中的三人或四人）踏上滑雪板，驾着狗和驯鹿拉的雪橇，向北极发起冲刺。
上一次探险，即『尼克斯』号（*Nix*）那次，还有另外几次，用的也是这个方案。*博雷尔*号与『尼克斯』号及其他船不同之处仅在于：她的设计更精巧，筹划得更周密。
一路平安无事，直到7月底我们遇上了一片浮冰漂流。8月1日抵达卡巴罗瓦，在那里同我们的运煤船会合，为应急加了一点煤——我们真正的动力是液体空气；还带上了四十三条狗、四头驯鹿和一批驯鹿苔；两天之后，我们终于掉转船头向北向东，在凛冽的天气里借着船帆和液体空气穿过厚重的「松散」冰区，直到8月27日，我们停泊在荒凉的泰梅尔岛外的一块浮冰旁。
我们在这里见到的头一样东西，是岸上一头正在守候小白鲑的熊：于是克拉克、缪和兰伯恩（Lamburn，轮机长）立刻乘大艇上岸，我和梅特兰乘小平底船随后跟上，每队带三条狗。

===Original===

It was while climbing away inland that Maitland said to me:
“When Clark leaves the ship for the dash to the Pole, it is three, not two, of us, after all, that he is going to take with him, making a party of four.”
*I*: “Is that so? Who knows?”
*Maitland*: “Wilson does. Clark has let it out in conversation with Wilson.”
*I*: “Well, the more the merrier. Who will be the three?”
*Maitland*: “Wilson is sure to be in it, and there may be Mew, making the third. As to the fourth, I suppose *I* shall get left out in the cold.”
*I*: “More likely I.”
*Maitland*: “Well, the race is between us four: Wilson, Mew, you and I. It is a question of physical fitness combined with special knowledge. You are too lucky a dog to get left out, Jeffson.”
*I*: “Well, what does it matter, so long as the expedition as a whole is successful? That is the main thing.”
*Maitland*: “Oh yes, that’s all very fine talk, Jeffson! But is it quite sincere? Isn’t it rather a pose to affect to despise $175,000,000? *I* want to be in at the death, and I mean to be, if I can. We are all more or less self-interested.”
“Look,” I whispered﻿—“a bear.”
It was a mother and cub: and with determined trudge she came wagging her low head, having no doubt smelled the dogs. We separated on the instant, doubling different ways behind ice-boulders, wanting her to go on nearer the shore, before killing; but, passing close, she spied, and bore down at a trot upon me. I fired into her neck, and at once, with a roar, she turned tail, making now straight in Maitland’s direction. I saw him run out from cover some hundred yards away, aiming his long-gun: but no report followed: and in half a minute he was under her forepaws, she striking out slaps at the barking, shrinking dogs. Maitland roared for my help: and at that moment, I, poor wretch, in far worse plight than he, stood shivering in ague: for suddenly one of those wrangles of the voices of my destiny was filling my bosom with loud commotion, one urging me to fly to Maitland’s aid, one passionately commanding me be still. But it lasted, I believe, some seconds only: I ran and got a shot into the bear’s brain, and Maitland leapt up with a rent down his face.
But singular destiny! Whatever I did﻿—if I did evil, if I did good﻿—the result was the same: tragedy dark and sinister!

===Chinese===

正是在往内陆攀行的时候，梅特兰对我说：
『等克拉克弃船向北极冲刺的时候，他终究要带上三个，而不是两个同伴，凑成一支四人队伍。』
*我*：『是吗？谁说得准？』
*梅特兰*：『威尔逊就知道。克拉克跟威尔逊聊天时说漏了嘴。』
*我*：『好啊，人多热闹嘛。会是哪三个？』
*梅特兰*：『威尔逊肯定在数，也许还有缪，凑成第三个。至于第四个，我看*我*要被晾在一边了。』
*我*：『更可能晾的是我。』
*梅特兰*：『嗯，竞赛就在我们四个之间：威尔逊、缪、你和我。这要看体格加上专门学识。你这家伙运气太好，晾不到你，杰夫森。』
*我*：『那有什么要紧，只要整个探险成功不就得了？那才是主要的。』
*梅特兰*：『哦，是呀，话说得漂亮，杰夫森！可这话当真吗？摆出一副看轻一亿七千五百万美元的样子，岂不有点做作？*我*可要在最后一刻在场，只要办得到，我就非在场不可。我们多多少少都自私。』
『看，』我低声道——『一头熊。』
那是一头母熊带着幼崽：她无疑嗅到了狗的气味，低垂着头一摇一摆，执拗地迈步走来。我们即刻散开，各自绕到冰砾背后，想让她再走近些、靠近岸边些再下手；可她擦身经过时发现了我们，小跑着朝我直冲过来。我朝她脖颈开了一枪，她立刻吼叫着掉头就跑，这下径直朝梅特兰的方向去了。我看见他从百来码外的掩蔽处冲出来，端起长枪瞄准：却没有枪声响起：不出半分钟，他已被她压在前掌之下，她左右开弓，拍打着吠叫退缩的狗。梅特兰吼着叫我救命：而那一刻，我这可怜虫，处境比他糟得多，站在那里如患疟疾般浑身发抖：因为我的命运之声忽然又争吵起来，一时间我胸中轰然大乱，一个催我飞奔去救梅特兰，一个激切地命我不许动。但我相信那只持续了几秒钟：我跑过去一枪射进熊的脑子，梅特兰跳了起来，脸上挂着一道口子。
可是奇特的命运啊！无论我做什么——行恶也罢，行善也罢——结果总是一样：黑暗而不祥的悲剧！

===Original===

Poor Maitland was doomed that voyage, and my rescue of his life was the means employed to make his death the more certain.
I think that I have already written, some pages back, about a man called Scotland, whom I met at Cambridge. He was always talking about certain “Black” and “White” beings, and their contention for the earth. We others used to call him the black-and-white mystery-man, because, one day﻿—but that is no matter now. Well, with regard to all that, I have a fancy, a whim of the mind﻿—quite wide of the truth, no doubt﻿—but I have it here in my brain, and I will write it down now. It is this: that there may have been some sort of arrangement, or understanding, between Black and White, as in the case of Adam and the fruit, that, should mankind force his way to the Pole and the old forbidden secret biding there, then some mishap should not fail to overtake the race of man; that the White, being kindly disposed to mankind, did not wish this to occur, and intended, for the sake of the race, to destroy our entire expedition before it reached; and that the Black, knowing that the White meant to do this, and by what means, used me﻿—*me*!﻿—to outwit this design, first of all working that I should be one of the party of four to leave the ship on ski.
But the childish attempt, my God, to read the immense riddle of the world! I could laugh loud at myself, and at poor Black-and-White Scotland, too. The thing can’t be so simple.
Well, we left Taimur the same day, and goodbye now to both land and open sea. Till we passed the latitude of Cape Chelyuskin (which we did not sight), it was one succession of ice-belts, with Mew in the crow’s-nest tormenting the electric bell to the engine-room, the anchor hanging ready to drop, and Clark taking soundings. Progress was slow, and the Polar night gathered round us apace, as we stole still onward and onward into that blue and glimmering land of eternal frore. We now left off bed-coverings of reindeer-skin and took to sleeping-bags. Eight of the dogs had died by the 25th September, when we were experiencing 19° of frost. In the darkest part of our night, the Northern Light spread its silent solemn banner over us, quivering round the heavens in a million fickle gauds.
The relations between the members of our little crew were excellent﻿—with one exception: David Wilson and I were not good friends.

===Chinese===

可怜的梅特兰在那次航程里是注定了的，而我对他性命的搭救，恰恰被用来使他的死更加笃定。
我想在前文若干页之前，我已经写过我在剑桥遇到的一个名叫斯科特兰（Scotland）的人。他老是谈论什么『黑』与『白』的存在，谈论它们对大地的争夺。我们大伙儿惯常叫他黑白之谜先生，因为有一天——不过如今这些都无关紧要了。嗯，关于这一切，我心里有一个怪想头，一念之痴——无疑与真相相去甚远——但它就在我脑子里，我现在要把它写下来。是这样的：黑与白之间也许有过某种约定，或者说默契，一如当年亚当与禁果之事——倘若人类硬要闯到北极、闯到那守候在那里的古老的禁秘跟前，那么某种灾祸便必定要降临人类；白的那位对人类心存善意，不愿此事发生，打算为了人类起见，让我们整支探险队在到达之前就全军覆没；而黑的那位知道白的要这么干，也知道他要用什么手段，便利用了我——利用*我*！——来挫败这一图谋，头一步便是设法让我成为踏滑雪板离船的四人之一。
可是我的天，这是何等孩子气的企图，竟想解读世界这巨大的谜！我真可以放声嘲笑我自己，也嘲笑可怜的黑白之谜斯科特兰。事情不可能这么简单。
嗯，我们当天就离开了泰梅尔，从此别了陆地，也别了开阔的海面。直到越过切柳斯金角的纬度（我们并没有望见那海角），一路上是一重接一重的冰带：缪在桅斗里不住地摁着通往机舱的电铃，锚悬着随时可落，克拉克一路测深。行进缓慢，极夜迅速地在我们四周合拢，我们悄悄地不断前行，进入那片蓝色的、微光闪烁的永恒冻土之境。这时我们撇下了驯鹿皮的铺盖，改用睡袋。到9月25日，狗已经死了八条，那时我们正经受零下19度的严寒。在我们黑夜最深沉的时分，北极光将它无声而庄严的旌旗在我们头顶展开，以百万种变幻的华彩在天宇四面震颤。
我们小小一班船员彼此的关系极好——只有一处例外：大卫·威尔逊（David Wilson）和我不睦。

===Original===

There was a something﻿—a tone﻿—in the evidence which he had given at the inquest on Peters, which made me mad every time I thought of it. He had heard Peters admit just before death that he, Peters, had administered atropine to himself: and he had had to give evidence of that fact. But he had given it in a most halfhearted way, so much so, that the coroner had asked him: “What, sir, are you hiding from me?” Wilson had replied: “Nothing. I have nothing to tell.”
And from that day he and I had hardly exchanged ten words, in spite of our constant companionship in the vessel; and one day, standing alone on a floe, I found myself hissing with clenched fist: “If he dared suspect Clodagh of poisoning Peters, I could *kill* him!”
Up to 78° of latitude the weather had been superb, but on the night of the 7th October﻿—well I remember it﻿—we experienced a great storm. Our tub of a ship rolled like a swing, drenching the whimpering dogs at every lurch, and hurling everything on board into confusion. The petroleum-launch was washed from the davits; down at one time to 40° below zero sank the thermometer; while a high aurora was whiffed into a dishevelled chaos of hues, resembling the smeared palette of some turbulent painter of the skies, or mixed battle of long-robed seraphim, and looking the very symbol of tribulation, tempest, wreck, and distraction. I, for the first time, was sick.
It was with a dizzy brain, therefore, that I went off watch to my bunk. Soon, indeed, I fell asleep: but the rolls and shocks of the ship, combined with the heavy Greenland anorak which I had on, and the state of my body, together produced a fearful nightmare, in which I was conscious of a vain struggle to move, a vain fight for breath, for the sleeping-bag turned to an iceberg on my bosom. Of Clodagh was my gasping dream. I dreamed that she let fall, drop by drop, a liquid, coloured like pomegranate-seeds, into a glass of water; and she presented the glass to Peters. The draught, I knew, was poisonous as death: and in a last effort to break the bands of that dark slumber, I was conscious, as I jerked myself upright, of screaming aloud:
“Clodagh! Clodagh! *Spare the man﻿ ﻿… !*”
My eyes, starting with horror, opened to waking; the electric light was shining in the cabin; and there stood David Wilson looking at me.

===Chinese===

在彼得斯死因研讯上他所作的证词里，有某种东西——某种腔调——我一想起来就发狂。他听过彼得斯临死前亲口承认，是他彼得斯自己给自己注射了阿托品：他不得不就这一事实作证。可他作证时有气无力，敷衍到了这地步，验尸官都问他：『先生，您有什么瞒着我？』威尔逊答道：『没有。我没有什么可说的。』
从那天起，尽管同船朝夕相处，我们俩几乎没说过十句话；有一天，我独自站在一块浮冰上，发觉自己攥紧拳头嘶嘶地咒道：『他敢疑心是克洛达毒死了彼得斯，我就*宰*了他！』
直到北纬78度，天气一直极好，可是10月7日夜里——我记得清清楚楚——我们遇上了一场大风暴。我们这条木盆似的船像秋千一样摇晃，每一次倾侧都把呜咽的狗浇个透湿，把船上的一切摔得乱七八糟。石油小艇从吊柱上被冲走了；温度计一度跌到零下40度；而高天的极光被吹成一团散乱的色乱，像某位狂躁的天空画家用脏了的调色板，又像一场长袍撒拉弗的混战，看上去正是苦难、风暴、毁灭与迷乱的化身。我生平第一次晕船了。
于是我晕头晕脑地下了岗，钻进自己的铺位。不久我果真睡着了：可船的翻滚颠簸，加上我身上那件沉重的格陵兰皮袄，加上我身体的状态，一起酿成了一场可怕的噩梦：梦里我挣扎着想动，徒劳地喘不上气，因为睡袋变成了一座压在我胸口的冰山。我喘息的梦全是克洛达。我梦见她把一种液体一滴一滴落入一杯水里，那液体的颜色像石榴籽；然后她把杯子递给彼得斯。我知道那杯东西毒得要命：于是我拼命想挣脱那黑暗睡眠的束缚，猛地把自己拽直身子时，我意识到自己在大声尖叫：
『克洛达！克洛达！*饶了那个人……！*』
我惊骇的眼睛睁开了，人已醒来；电灯照着船舱；大卫·威尔逊正站在那里看着我。

===Original===

Wilson was a big man, with a massively-built, long face, made longer by a beard, and he had little nervous contractions of the flesh at the cheekbones, and plenty of big freckles. His clinging pose, his smile of disgust, his whole air, as he stood crouching and lurching there, I can shut my eyes, and see now.
What he was doing in my cabin I did not know. To think, my good God, that he should have been led there just then! This was one of the four-men starboard berths: *his* was a-port: yet there he was! But he explained at once.
“Sorry to interrupt your innocent dreams,” says he: “the mercury in Maitland’s thermometer is frozen, and he asked me to hand him his spirits-of-wine one from his bunk﻿ ﻿…”
I did not answer. A hatred was in my heart against this man.
The next day the storm died away, and either three or four days later the slush-ice between the floes froze definitely. The *Boreal*’s way was thus blocked. We warped her with ice-anchors and the capstan into the position in which she should lay up for her winter’s drift. This was in about 79° 20′ N. The sun had now totally vanished from our bleak sky, not to reappear till the following year.
Well, there was sledging with the dogs, and bear-hunting among the hummocks, as the months, one by one, went by. One day Wilson, by far our best shot, got a walrus-bull; Clark followed the traditional pursuit of a Chief, examining Crustacea; Maitland and I were in a relation of close friendship, and I assisted his meteorological observations in a snow-hut built near the ship. Often, through the twenty-four hours, a clear blue moon, very spectral, very fair, suffused all our dim and livid clime.
It was five days before Christmas that Clark made the great announcement: he had determined, he said, if our splendid northward drift continued, to leave the ship about the middle of next March for the dash to the Pole. He would take with him the four reindeer, all the dogs, four sledges, four kayaks, and three companions. The companions whom he had decided to invite were: Wilson, Mew, and Maitland.
He said it at dinner; and as he said it, David Wilson glanced at my wan face with a smile of pleased malice: for *I* was left out.
I remember well: the aurora that night was in the sky, and at its edge floated a moon surrounded by a ring, with two mock-moons.

===Chinese===

威尔逊是个大块头，长脸厚重，蓄了胡须显得更长，颧骨上的皮肉不时神经质地抽动，脸上还有许多大雀斑。他站在那儿佝偻着、随着船身摇晃时那副紧攀的姿势、那厌恶的微笑、那整个神态，我如今闭上眼睛还能看见。
他在我舱里做什么，我并不知道。想想看，我的老天，偏偏那个时候他被引到那里去了！这是右舷四人舱之一：*他的*铺在左舷：可他偏偏站在那儿！不过他立刻作了解释。
『抱歉打断了你无辜的美梦，』他说，『梅特兰温度计里的水银冻住了，他叫我把他那支酒精的从铺位上递给他……』
我没有回答。我心里恨这个人。
第二天风暴平息了，大约三四天后，浮冰之间的雪泥冰彻底冻结了。*博雷尔*号的去路就此堵死。我们用冰锚和绞盘把她绞进她该停泊过冬漂流的位置。这里约在北纬79度20分。太阳这时已从我们荒凉的天空中完全隐没，要到第二年才会重现。
嗯，随着月份一个个过去，我们驾狗拉雪橇，在冰丘间猎熊。有一天，威尔逊——我们船上枪法最好的——打到一头公海象；克拉克则沿袭队长的传统消遣，考察甲壳动物；梅特兰和我交情甚密，我在船旁建的一座雪屋里协助他做气象观测。二十四小时里，常常一轮碧蓝的月亮，幽灵似的，明艳极了，漫洒在我们这昏暗青灰的天地间。
圣诞节前五天，克拉克宣布了那件大事：他说，倘若我们漂亮的北向漂流持续下去，他已决定于来年三月中旬弃船出发，向北极冲刺。他将带上那四头驯鹿、所有的狗、四架雪橇、四条皮划艇，以及三名同伴。他定下的人选是：威尔逊、缪和梅特兰。
他是在晚餐桌上说的；话音落处，大卫·威尔逊瞥了瞥我苍白的脸，露出快意的恶笑：因为被晾下的是*我*。
我记得清楚：那夜天上有极光，极光边缘浮着一轮带光环的月亮，旁边还有两个幻月。

===Original===

But all shone very vaguely and far, and a fog, which had already lasted some days, made the ship’s bows indistinct to me, as I paced the bridge on my watch, two hours after Clark’s announcement.
For a long time all was very still, save for the occasional whine of a dog. I was alone, and it grew toward the end of my watch, when Maitland would succeed me. My slow tread tolled like a passing-bell, and the mountainous ice lay vague and white around me, its sheeted ghastliness not less dreadfully silent than eternity itself.
Presently, several of the dogs began barking together, left off, and began again.
I said to myself; “There is a bear about somewhere.”
And after some five minutes I saw﻿—I thought that I saw﻿—it. The fog had, if anything thickened; and it was now very near the end of my watch.
It had entered the ship, I concluded, by the boards which slanted from an opening in the port bulwarks down to the ice. Once before, in November, a bear, having smelled the dogs, had ventured on board at midnight: but *then* there had resulted a perfect hubbub among the dogs. *Now*, even in the midst of my excitement, I wondered at their quietness, though some whimpered﻿—with fear, I thought. I saw the creature steal forward from the hatchway toward the kennels a-port; and I ran noiselessly, and seized the watch-gun which stood always loaded by the companionway.
By this time, the form had passed the kennels, reached the bows, and now was making toward me on the starboard side. I took aim. Never, I thought, had I seen so huge a bear﻿—though I made allowance for the magnifying effect of the fog.
My finger was on the trigger: and at that moment a deathly shivering sickness took me, the wrangling voices shouted at me, with “Shoot!” “Shoot not!” “Shoot!” Ah well, that latter shout was irresistible. I drew the trigger. The report hooted through the Polar night.
The creature dropped; both Wilson and Clark were up at once: and we three hurried to the spot.
But the very first near glance showed a singular kind of bear. Wilson put his hand to the head, and a lax skin came away at his touch.﻿ ﻿… It was Aubrey Maitland who was underneath it, and I had shot him dead.
For the past few days he had been cleaning skins, among them the skin of the bear from which I had saved him at Taimur.

===Chinese===

但一切都照得朦胧而遥远，一场已经持续了几天的雾，把船头也弄得影影绰绰——那是在克拉克宣布之后两小时，我在桥楼上值更踱步的时候。
好一阵子万籁俱寂，只有偶尔一声狗的呜咽。只有我一人，快到我交更的时候了，梅特兰就要来接班。我缓慢的脚步像丧钟一样敲着，山岳般的冰在我四周朦胧地白着，它那裹尸布似的惨白，死寂得不下于永恒本身。
过了一会儿，几条狗一齐吠起来，停了，又吠起来。
我自语道：『哪儿有头熊在转悠。』
约莫五分钟后，我看见了——我以为我看见了——它。雾如果有什么变化，那就是更浓了；而这时已很接近我交更的时刻。
我推断，它是从左舷舷墙一处开口斜搭到冰上的跳板上了船。先前11月里有一回，一头熊闻到了狗的气味，半夜斗胆上了船：可*那时*狗群闹得天翻地覆。*如今*，即使在我激动之际，我也诧异它们竟这样安静，虽然有几条在呜咽——我猜是吓的。我看见那东西从舱口悄悄朝左舷的狗窝挪去；我悄没声儿地跑过去，抓起一直上着子弹立在舱梯旁的值更枪。
这时，那影子已过了狗窝，到了船头，正朝我右舷这边过来。我举枪瞄准。我想，从没见过这么大的熊——虽然我也把雾的放大作用考虑了进去。
我的手指已扣在扳机上：就在那一刻，一阵死一般的寒噤袭来，那争吵的声音朝我呐喊：『开枪！』『不许开枪！』『开枪！』唉，后一声喊是无法抗拒的。我扣动了扳机。枪声呼啸着划过极夜。
那东西倒了下去；威尔逊和克拉克立刻都起来了：我们三人赶到那里。
可是走近头一眼便看出这是一头奇特的熊。威尔逊伸手摸向那脑袋，一搭手，一张松垮的皮就整个褪了下来。……皮底下是奥布里·梅特兰，我一枪把他打死了。
前几天他一直在整理兽皮，其中就有我在泰梅尔救下他那回的那头熊的皮。

===Original===

Now, Maitland was a born pantomimist, continually inventing practical jokes; and perhaps to startle me with a false alarm in the very skin of the old Bruin which had so nearly done for him, he had thrown it round him on finishing its cleaning, and so, in mere wanton fun, had crept on deck at the hour of his watch. The head of the bearskin, and the fog, must have prevented him from seeing me taking aim.
This tragedy made me ill for weeks. I saw that the hand of Fate was upon me. When I rose from bed, poor Maitland was lying in the ice behind the great camel-shaped hummock near us.
By the end of January we had drifted to 80° 55′; and it was then that Clark, in the presence of Wilson, asked me if I would make the fourth man, in the place of poor Maitland, for the dash in the spring. As I said “Yes, I am willing,” David Wilson spat with a disgusted emphasis. A minute later he sighed, with “Ah, poor Maitland﻿ ﻿…” and drew in his breath with a *tut! tut!*
God knows, I had an impulse to spring then and there at his throat, and strangle him: but I curbed myself.
There remained now hardly a month before the dash, and all hands set to work with a will, measuring the dogs, making harness and sealskin shoes for them, overhauling sledges and kayaks, and cutting every possible ounce of weight. But we were not destined, after all, to set out that year. About the 20th February, the ice began to pack, and the ship was subjected to an appalling pressure. We found it necessary to make trumpets of our hands to shout into one another’s ears, for the whole ice-continent was crashing, popping, thundering everywhere in terrific upheaval. Expecting every moment to see the *Boreal* crushed to splinters, we had to set about unpacking provisions, and placing sledges, kayaks, dogs and everything in a position for instant flight. It lasted five days, and was accompanied by a tempest from the north, which, by the end of February, had driven us back south into latitude 79° 40′. Clark, of course, then abandoned the thought of the Pole for that summer.
And immediately afterwards we made a startling discovery: our stock of reindeer-moss was found to be somehow ridiculously small. Egan, our second mate, was blamed; but that did not help matters: the sad fact remained.

===Chinese===
